[](/wiki/List_of_coats_of_arms_of_Prime_Ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom "List of coats of arms of Prime Ministers of the United Kingdom")  
  
  * [v](/wiki/Template:Margaret_Thatcher_sidebar "Template:Margaret Thatcher sidebar")
  * [t](/wiki/Template_talk:Margaret_Thatcher_sidebar "Template talk:Margaret Thatcher sidebar")
  * [e](/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:Margaret_Thatcher_sidebar "Special:EditPage/Template:Margaret Thatcher sidebar")

  
  
**Margaret Hilda Thatcher, Baroness Thatcher**[nb 2] ([née](/wiki/Birth_name#Maiden_and_married_names "Birth name") **Roberts** ; 13 October 1925 - 8 April 2013), was a British stateswoman who served as [Prime Minister of the United Kingdom](/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom "Prime Minister of the United Kingdom") from 1979 to 1990 and [Leader of the Conservative Party](/wiki/Leader_of_the_Conservative_Party_\(UK\) "Leader of the Conservative Party \(UK\)") from 1975 to 1990. She was the [longest-serving British prime minister](/wiki/List_of_prime_ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom_by_length_of_tenure "List of prime ministers of the United Kingdom by length of tenure") of the 20th century and the first woman to hold the office. As prime minister, she implemented policies that came to be known as [Thatcherism](/wiki/Thatcherism "Thatcherism"). A Soviet journalist dubbed her the "**Iron Lady** ", a nickname that became associated with her uncompromising politics and [leadership style](/wiki/Leadership_style "Leadership style"). 

Thatcher studied chemistry at [Somerville College, Oxford](/wiki/Somerville_College,_Oxford "Somerville College, Oxford"), and worked briefly as a [research chemist](/wiki/Research_chemist "Research chemist") before becoming a [barrister](/wiki/Barristers_in_England_and_Wales "Barristers in England and Wales"). She was [elected Member of Parliament](/wiki/List_of_MPs_elected_in_the_1959_United_Kingdom_general_election "List of MPs elected in the 1959 United Kingdom general election") for [Finchley](/wiki/Finchley_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\) "Finchley \(UK Parliament constituency\)") in [1959](/wiki/1959_United_Kingdom_general_election "1959 United Kingdom general election"). [Edward Heath](/wiki/Edward_Heath "Edward Heath") appointed her [Secretary of State for Education and Science](/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Education_and_Science "Secretary of State for Education and Science") in [his 1970–1974 government](/wiki/Heath_ministry "Heath ministry"). In 1975 she defeated Heath in the [Conservative Party leadership election](/wiki/1975_Conservative_Party_leadership_election "1975 Conservative Party leadership election") to become [Leader of the Opposition](/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_\(United_Kingdom\) "Leader of the Opposition \(United Kingdom\)") and the first woman to lead a major British political party. 

On becoming prime minister after winning the [1979 general election](/wiki/1979_United_Kingdom_general_election "1979 United Kingdom general election"), Thatcher introduced a series of economic policies intended to reverse high inflation and Britain's struggles in the wake of the [Winter of Discontent](/wiki/Winter_of_Discontent "Winter of Discontent") and [an oncoming recession](/wiki/Early_1980s_recession "Early 1980s recession").[nb 3] Her political philosophy and economic policies emphasised [greater individual liberty](/wiki/Libertarian_conservatism "Libertarian conservatism"), the privatisation of [state-owned companies](/wiki/State-owned_companies "State-owned companies"), and reducing the power and influence of [trade unions](/wiki/Trade_unions_in_the_United_Kingdom "Trade unions in the United Kingdom"). Her popularity in her first years in office waned amid the recession and rising unemployment. Victory in the 1982 [Falklands War](/wiki/Falklands_War "Falklands War") and the recovering economy brought a resurgence of support, resulting in the [Conservative Party](/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\) "Conservative Party \(UK\)") being [returned to government](/wiki/1983_United_Kingdom_general_election "1983 United Kingdom general election") in a [landslide victory](/wiki/Landslide_victory "Landslide victory") in 1983. She survived an assassination attempt by the [Provisional IRA](/wiki/Provisional_IRA "Provisional IRA") in the 1984 [Brighton hotel bombing](/wiki/Brighton_hotel_bombing "Brighton hotel bombing") and achieved a political victory against the [National Union of Mineworkers](/wiki/National_Union_of_Mineworkers_\(Great_Britain\) "National Union of Mineworkers \(Great Britain\)") in the [1984–85 miners' strike](/wiki/1984%E2%80%9385_miners%27_strike "1984–85 miners' strike"). In 1986 Thatcher oversaw the [deregulation](/wiki/Deregulation "Deregulation") of UK [financial markets](/wiki/Financial_market "Financial market"), leading to [an economic boom](/wiki/Lawson_Boom "Lawson Boom"), in what came to be known as the [Big Bang](/wiki/Big_Bang_\(financial_markets\) "Big Bang \(financial markets\)"). 

Thatcher was re-elected for a third term with another landslide [in 1987](/wiki/1987_United_Kingdom_general_election "1987 United Kingdom general election"), but her subsequent support for the [Community Charge](/wiki/Community_Charge "Community Charge") (also known as the "poll tax") was widely unpopular, and her increasingly [Eurosceptic](/wiki/Euroscepticism_in_the_United_Kingdom "Euroscepticism in the United Kingdom") views on the [European Community](/wiki/European_Community "European Community") were not shared by others in her cabinet. She resigned as prime minister and party leader in 1990, after a [challenge was launched to her leadership](/wiki/1990_Conservative_Party_leadership_election "1990 Conservative Party leadership election"), and was succeeded by [John Major](/wiki/John_Major "John Major"), her [Chancellor of the Exchequer](/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer "Chancellor of the Exchequer").[nb 4] After retiring from the [House of Commons](/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom "House of Commons of the United Kingdom") in 1992, she was given a [life peerage](/wiki/Life_peer "Life peer"), which entitled her to sit in the [House of Lords](/wiki/House_of_Lords "House of Lords"). In 2013 [she died](/wiki/Death_and_funeral_of_Margaret_Thatcher "Death and funeral of Margaret Thatcher") of a stroke at [the Ritz Hotel, London](/wiki/The_Ritz_Hotel,_London "The Ritz Hotel, London"), at the age of 87. 

A polarising figure in British politics, Thatcher is nonetheless viewed favourably in [historical rankings](/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom "Historical rankings of prime ministers of the United Kingdom") and public opinion of British prime ministers. Her tenure constituted a [realignment](/wiki/Political_realignment#Europe "Political realignment") towards [neoliberal](/wiki/Neoliberal "Neoliberal") policies in Britain; the complex legacy attributed to this shift continues to be debated into the 21st century. 

## Early life and education

Birthplace in Grantham[](/wiki/File:Maison_natale_de_Margaret_Thatcher,_Grantham.JPG)2009 photograph of her father's former shop[4][](/wiki/File:Plaque,_maison_natale_de_Margaret_Thatcher.JPG)Commemorative plaque[5]Margaret and her elder sister were raised in the bottom of two flats on North Parade.[6]  
---  
  
### Family and childhood (1925–1943)

Margaret Hilda Roberts was born on 13 October 1925 in [Grantham](/wiki/Grantham "Grantham"), Lincolnshire.[7] Her parents were [Alfred Roberts](/wiki/Alfred_Roberts "Alfred Roberts") (1892–1970), from [Northamptonshire](/wiki/Northamptonshire "Northamptonshire"), and Beatrice Ethel Stephenson (1888–1960), from Lincolnshire.[8] Her father's maternal grandmother, Catherine Sullivan, was born in [County Kerry](/wiki/County_Kerry "County Kerry"), [Ireland](/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland "United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland").[9]

Roberts spent her childhood in Grantham, where her father owned a [tobacconist](/wiki/Tobacconist "Tobacconist")'s and a grocery shop. In 1938, [before the Second World War](/wiki/Events_preceding_World_War_II_in_Europe "Events preceding World War II in Europe"), the Roberts family briefly gave sanctuary to a teenage Jewish girl who had [escaped Nazi Germany](/wiki/Jewish_refugees_from_German-occupied_Europe_in_the_United_Kingdom "Jewish refugees from German-occupied Europe in the United Kingdom"). With her [pen-friending](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/penfriend#English "wikt:penfriend") elder sister Muriel, Margaret saved pocket money to help pay for the teenager's journey.[10]

Alfred was an [alderman](/wiki/Alderman "Alderman") and a [Methodist local preacher](/wiki/Methodist_local_preacher "Methodist local preacher").[11] He brought up his daughter as a strict [Wesleyan Methodist](/wiki/Wesleyan_Methodist_Church_\(Great_Britain\) "Wesleyan Methodist Church \(Great Britain\)"),[12] attending the [Finkin Street Methodist Church](/wiki/Finkin_Street_Methodist_Church "Finkin Street Methodist Church"),[13] but Margaret was more sceptical; the future scientist told a friend that she could not believe in [angels](/wiki/Angel "Angel"), having calculated that they needed a [breastbone](/wiki/Breastbone "Breastbone") 6 feet (1.8 m) long to support wings.[14] Alfred came from a [Liberal](/wiki/Liberal_Party_\(UK\) "Liberal Party \(UK\)") family but stood (as was then customary in local government) as an [Independent](/wiki/Independent_politician "Independent politician"). He served as Mayor of Grantham from 1945 to 1946 and lost his position as alderman in 1952 after the [Labour Party](/wiki/Labour_Party_\(UK\) "Labour Party \(UK\)") won its first majority on Grantham Council in 1950.[11]

[](/wiki/File:Margaret_Thatcher_aos_13_anos_de_idade.jpg)1938–39 portrait, aged 13

Roberts attended [Huntingtower Road Primary School](/wiki/Huntingtower_Road_Primary_School "Huntingtower Road Primary School") and won a scholarship to [Kesteven and Grantham Girls' School](/wiki/Kesteven_and_Grantham_Girls%27_School "Kesteven and Grantham Girls' School"), a grammar school.[15] Her school reports showed hard work and continual improvement; her extracurricular activities included the piano, field hockey, poetry recitals, swimming and walking.[16] She was [head girl](/wiki/Head_girl "Head girl") in 1942–43,[17] and outside school, while the Second World War was ongoing, she voluntarily worked as a [fire watcher](/wiki/Fire_watcher "Fire watcher") in the local [ARP service](/wiki/ARP_service "ARP service").[18] Other students thought of Roberts as the "star scientist", although mistaken advice regarding cleaning ink from [parquetry](/wiki/Parquetry "Parquetry") almost caused [chlorine gas poisoning](/wiki/Chlorine_gas_poisoning "Chlorine gas poisoning"). In her [upper sixth year](/wiki/Sixth_form "Sixth form"), Roberts was accepted for a scholarship to study chemistry at [Somerville College, Oxford](/wiki/Somerville_College,_Oxford "Somerville College, Oxford"), a women's college, starting in 1944. After another candidate withdrew, Roberts entered Oxford in October 1943.[19][14]

### Oxford (1943–1947)

[](/wiki/File:Somerville_College.jpg)Roberts studied chemistry at [Somerville College](/wiki/Somerville_College "Somerville College") (_pictured_) from 1943 to 1947.

Following her arrival at Oxford, Roberts began studies under [X-ray crystallographer](/wiki/X-ray_crystallographer "X-ray crystallographer") [Dorothy Hodgkin](/wiki/Dorothy_Hodgkin "Dorothy Hodgkin"), the tutor in chemistry for Somerville College since 1934.[20][21] Hodgkin considered Roberts a "good" student, and later recalled that "[o]ne could always rely on her producing a sensible, well-read essay".[22] She opted to read for a classified [honours degree](/wiki/Honours_degree "Honours degree"), entailing an additional year of supervised research.[22] As her thesis supervisor, Hodgkin assigned Roberts to work with [Gerhard Schmidt](/wiki/Gerhard_Schmidt_\(crystallographer\) "Gerhard Schmidt \(crystallographer\)"), a researcher in Hodgkin's lab, to determine the structure of the antibiotic [peptide](/wiki/Peptide "Peptide") [gramicidin S](/wiki/Gramicidin_S "Gramicidin S").[23] Although the research made some progress, the peptide's structure proved more complex than anticipated, and Schmidt would only determine its full structure much later; Roberts (by then Thatcher) learned this in the 1960s while visiting the [Weizmann Institute](/wiki/Weizmann_Institute "Weizmann Institute"), where her former research partner was then working.[22]

Roberts graduated in 1947 with a [second-class honours degree](/wiki/British_undergraduate_degree_classification "British undergraduate degree classification") in chemistry, and in 1950 also received the degree of [Master of Arts](/wiki/Master_of_Arts_\(Oxford,_Cambridge,_and_Dublin\) "Master of Arts \(Oxford, Cambridge, and Dublin\)") (as an Oxford BA, she was entitled to the degree 21 terms after her [matriculation](/wiki/Matriculation#United_Kingdom "Matriculation")).[24] Although Hodgkin would later be critical of her former student's politics, they continued to correspond into the 1980s, and Roberts in her memoirs would describe her mentor as "ever-helpful", "a brilliant scientist and a gifted teacher".[22] As prime minister, she would keep a portrait of Hodgkin at [10 Downing Street](/wiki/10_Downing_Street "10 Downing Street").[22] Later in life, she was reportedly prouder of becoming the first prime minister with a science degree than becoming the first female prime minister.[25] While prime minister, she attempted to preserve Somerville as a women's college.[26] Twice a week outside study, she worked in a local forces canteen.[27]

During her time at Oxford, Roberts was noted for her isolated and serious attitude.[14] Her first boyfriend, Tony Bray (1926–2014), recalled that she was "very thoughtful and a very good conversationalist. That's probably what interested me. She was good at general subjects".[14][28]

She was a member of [Wesley Memorial Church](/wiki/Wesley_Memorial_Church "Wesley Memorial Church"), became a [lay preacher](/wiki/Lay_preacher "Lay preacher") and joined the John Wesley Society.[29][30] She also attended [Somerville College Chapel](/wiki/Somerville_College_Chapel "Somerville College Chapel") and [St Mary's](/wiki/University_Church_of_St_Mary_the_Virgin "University Church of St Mary the Virgin").[30]

Roberts's coursework involved subjects beyond chemistry[31] as she was already contemplating an entry into law and politics.[32] Her enthusiasm for politics as a girl made Bray think of her as "unusual" and her parents as "slightly austere" and "very proper".[14][28] Roberts became President of the [Oxford University Conservative Association](/wiki/Oxford_University_Conservative_Association "Oxford University Conservative Association") in 1946.[33] She was influenced at university by political works such as [Friedrich Hayek](/wiki/Friedrich_Hayek "Friedrich Hayek")'s _[The Road to Serfdom](/wiki/The_Road_to_Serfdom "The Road to Serfdom")_ (1944),[34] which condemned economic intervention by government as a precursor to an authoritarian state.[35]

### Post-Oxford career (1947–1951)

After graduating, Roberts secured a position as a research chemist for British Xylonite ([BX Plastics](/wiki/BX_Plastics "BX Plastics")) following a series of interviews arranged by Oxford; she subsequently moved to [Colchester](/wiki/Colchester "Colchester") in Essex to work at the firm.[36] Little is known about her brief time there.[37] By her own account, she was initially enthusiastic about the position, as she had been intended to function as a personal assistant to the company's head of research and development, providing opportunities to learn about [operations management](/wiki/Operations_management "Operations management"): "But on my arrival it was decided that there was not enough to do in that capacity."[22] Instead, she seems to have researched methods of attaching [polyvinyl chloride](/wiki/Polyvinyl_chloride "Polyvinyl chloride") (PVC) to metals.[37] While with the firm, she joined the [Association of Scientific Workers](/wiki/Association_of_Scientific_Workers "Association of Scientific Workers").[37] In 1948, she applied for a job at [Imperial Chemical Industries](/wiki/Imperial_Chemical_Industries "Imperial Chemical Industries") (ICI) but was rejected after the personnel department assessed her as "headstrong, obstinate and dangerously self-opinionated".[38] Jon Agar in _[Notes and Records](/wiki/Notes_and_Records "Notes and Records")_ argues that her understanding of modern scientific research later impacted her views as prime minister.[37]

Roberts joined the local [Conservative Association](/wiki/Conservative_Association "Conservative Association") and attended the party conference at [Llandudno](/wiki/Llandudno "Llandudno"), Wales, in 1948, as a representative of the University Graduate Conservative Association.[39] Meanwhile, she became a high-ranking affiliate of the [Vermin Club](/wiki/Vermin_Club "Vermin Club"),[40][41] a group of grassroots Conservatives formed in response to a derogatory comment made by [Aneurin Bevan](/wiki/Aneurin_Bevan "Aneurin Bevan").[41] One of her Oxford friends was also a friend of the Chair of the [Dartford](/wiki/Dartford "Dartford") Conservative Association in [Kent](/wiki/Kent "Kent"), who were looking for candidates.[39] Officials of the association were so impressed by her that they asked her to apply, even though she was not on the party's approved list; she was selected in January 1950 (aged 24) and added to the approved list [_post ante_](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/post_ante "wikt:post ante").[42]

At a dinner following her formal adoption as Conservative candidate for Dartford in February 1949, she met divorcé [Denis Thatcher](/wiki/Denis_Thatcher "Denis Thatcher"), a successful and wealthy businessman, who drove her to her Essex train.[43] After their first meeting, she described him to Muriel as "not a very attractive creature – very reserved but quite nice".[14] In preparation for the election, Roberts moved to Dartford, while she supported herself by working as a research chemist for [J. Lyons and Co.](/wiki/J._Lyons_and_Co. "J. Lyons and Co.") in [Hammersmith](/wiki/Hammersmith "Hammersmith"), reportedly as part of a team developing [emulsifiers](/wiki/Emulsifier "Emulsifier") for [ice cream](/wiki/Ice_cream "Ice cream").[44] As the work was more theoretical in nature than during her prior role with BX Plastics, Roberts found it "more satisfying".[22] While at Lyons, she worked under the supervision of Hans Jellinek, who headed the company's physical chemistry section.[45] Jellinek assigned her to research the [saponification](/wiki/Saponification "Saponification") of α-monostearin ([glycerol monostearate](/wiki/Glycerol_monostearate "Glycerol monostearate")), which has properties as an emulsifier, stabiliser and food preservative. Agar has noted the research may have been connected with the emulsification of ice cream, but only as a possibility.[22] In September 1951, their research was published in the _[Journal of the Science of Food and Agriculture](/wiki/Journal_of_the_Science_of_Food_and_Agriculture "Journal of the Science of Food and Agriculture")_ , a recently launched publication of the [Society of Chemical Industry](/wiki/Society_of_Chemical_Industry "Society of Chemical Industry"),[22] as "The saponification of α-monostearin in a monolayer".[46] This would be Roberts's sole scientific publication.[22] In 1979, following his former assistant's election as prime minister, Jellinek, by then a professor of physical chemistry at [Clarkson University](/wiki/Clarkson_University "Clarkson University") in the United States, said she had done "a very good job" on the project, "showing great determination".[47] She sent Jellinek a congratulatory letter upon his retirement in 1984, and another letter shortly before his death two years later.[48]

Roberts married at [Wesley's Chapel](/wiki/Wesley%27s_Chapel "Wesley's Chapel") and her children were baptised there,[49] but she and her husband began attending [Church of England](/wiki/Church_of_England "Church of England") services and would later convert to [Anglicanism](/wiki/Anglicanism "Anglicanism").[50][30]

## Early political career

In the [1950](/wiki/1950_United_Kingdom_general_election "1950 United Kingdom general election") and [1951](/wiki/1951_United_Kingdom_general_election "1951 United Kingdom general election") general elections, Roberts was the Conservative candidate for the Labour seat of [Dartford](/wiki/Dartford_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\) "Dartford \(UK Parliament constituency\)"). The local party selected her as its candidate because, though not a dynamic public speaker, Roberts was well-prepared and fearless in her answers. A prospective candidate, [Bill Deedes](/wiki/Bill_Deedes "Bill Deedes"), recalled: "Once she opened her mouth, the rest of us began to look rather second-rate."[25] She attracted media attention as the youngest and the only female candidate;[51] in 1950, she was the youngest Conservative candidate in the country.[52] She lost on both occasions to [Norman Dodds](/wiki/Norman_Dodds "Norman Dodds") but reduced the Labour majority by 6,000 and then a further 1,000.[53] During the campaigns, she was supported by her parents and by her future husband Denis Thatcher, whom she married in December 1951.[53][54] She trained at the [Inns of Court School of Law](/wiki/Inns_of_Court_School_of_Law "Inns of Court School of Law") (now part of The City Law School)[55] and qualified as a [barrister](/wiki/Barristers_in_England_and_Wales "Barristers in England and Wales") in 1953 (specialising in taxation).[56] Denis funded his wife's studies for the [bar](/wiki/Bar_association "Bar association").[57] Later that same year, their twins [Carol](/wiki/Carol_Thatcher "Carol Thatcher") and [Mark](/wiki/Mark_Thatcher "Mark Thatcher") were born, delivered prematurely by Caesarean section.[58]

### Member of Parliament (1959–1970)

In 1954, Thatcher was defeated when she sought selection to be the [Conservative Party](/wiki/Conservative_Party_\(UK\) "Conservative Party \(UK\)") candidate for the [Orpington by-election](/wiki/1955_Orpington_by-election "1955 Orpington by-election") of January 1955. She chose not to stand as a candidate in the [1955 general election](/wiki/1955_United_Kingdom_general_election "1955 United Kingdom general election"), in later years, stating: "I really just felt the twins were [...] only two, I really felt that it was too soon. I couldn't do that."[59] Afterwards, Thatcher began looking for a Conservative safe seat and was selected as the candidate for [Finchley](/wiki/Finchley_\(UK_Parliament_constituency\) "Finchley \(UK Parliament constituency\)") in April 1958 (narrowly beating [Ian Montagu Fraser](/wiki/Ian_Montagu_Fraser "Ian Montagu Fraser")). She was elected as MP for the seat after a hard campaign in the [1959 election](/wiki/1959_United_Kingdom_general_election "1959 United Kingdom general election").[60][61] Benefiting from her fortunate result in a lottery for [backbenchers](/wiki/Backbencher "Backbencher") to propose new legislation,[25] Thatcher's maiden speech was, unusually, in support of her [private member's bill](/wiki/Private_member%27s_bill "Private member's bill"), the [Public Bodies (Admission to Meetings) Act 1960](/wiki/Public_Bodies_\(Admission_to_Meetings\)_Act_1960 "Public Bodies \(Admission to Meetings\) Act 1960"), requiring local authorities to hold their council meetings in public; the bill was successful and became law.[62][63] In 1961 she went against the Conservative Party's official position by voting for the restoration of [birching](/wiki/Birching "Birching") as a [judicial corporal punishment](/wiki/Judicial_corporal_punishment "Judicial corporal punishment").[64]

#### On the frontbenches

Thatcher's talent and drive caused her to be mentioned as a future prime minister in her early 20s[25] although she herself was more pessimistic, stating as late as 1970: "There will not be a woman prime minister in my lifetime – the male population is too prejudiced."[65] In October 1961 she was promoted to the [frontbench](/wiki/Frontbench "Frontbench") as [Parliamentary Secretary to the Ministry for Pensions](/wiki/Parliamentary_Secretary_to_the_Ministry_for_Pensions "Parliamentary Secretary to the Ministry for Pensions") by [Harold Macmillan](/wiki/Harold_Macmillan "Harold Macmillan").[66] Thatcher was the youngest woman in history to receive such a post, and among the first [MPs elected in 1959](/wiki/List_of_MPs_elected_in_the_1959_United_Kingdom_general_election "List of MPs elected in the 1959 United Kingdom general election") to be promoted.[67] After the Conservatives lost the [1964 election](/wiki/1964_United_Kingdom_general_election "1964 United Kingdom general election"), she became spokeswoman on housing and land. In that position, she advocated her party's policy of giving tenants the [right to buy](/wiki/Right_to_buy "Right to buy") their [council houses](/wiki/Council_house "Council house").[68] She moved to the [Shadow Treasury](/wiki/Shadow_Treasury "Shadow Treasury") team in 1966 and, as Treasury spokeswoman, opposed Labour's mandatory price and income controls, arguing they would unintentionally produce effects that would distort the economy.[68]

[Jim Prior](/wiki/Jim_Prior "Jim Prior") suggested Thatcher as a [Shadow Cabinet](/wiki/Official_Opposition_Shadow_Cabinet_\(United_Kingdom\) "Official Opposition Shadow Cabinet \(United Kingdom\)") member after the Conservatives' [1966 defeat](/wiki/1966_United_Kingdom_general_election "1966 United Kingdom general election"), but party leader [Edward Heath](/wiki/Edward_Heath "Edward Heath") and Chief Whip [William Whitelaw](/wiki/William_Whitelaw "William Whitelaw") eventually chose [Mervyn Pike](/wiki/Mervyn_Pike "Mervyn Pike") as the [Conservative shadow cabinet](/wiki/First_Heath_shadow_cabinet "First Heath shadow cabinet")'s sole woman member.[67] At the 1966 Conservative Party conference, Thatcher criticised the high-tax policies of the [Labour government](/wiki/Labour_government,_1964%E2%80%931970 "Labour government, 1964–1970") as being steps "not only towards Socialism, but towards Communism", arguing that lower taxes served as an incentive to hard work.[68] Thatcher was one of the few Conservative MPs to support [Leo Abse](/wiki/Leo_Abse "Leo Abse")'s bill to decriminalise male homosexuality.[69] She voted in favour of [David Steel](/wiki/David_Steel "David Steel")'s bill to legalise abortion,[70][71] as well as a ban on [hare coursing](/wiki/Hare_coursing "Hare coursing").[72] She supported the retention of capital punishment[73] and voted against the relaxation of divorce laws.[74][75]

#### In the Shadow Cabinet

In 1967, the [United States Embassy](/wiki/Embassy_of_the_United_States,_London "Embassy of the United States, London") chose Thatcher to take part in the [International Visitor Leadership Program](/wiki/International_Visitor_Leadership_Program "International Visitor Leadership Program") (then called the Foreign Leader Program), a professional exchange programme that allowed her to spend about six weeks visiting various US cities and political figures as well as institutions such as the [International Monetary Fund](/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund "International Monetary Fund"). Although she was not yet a Shadow Cabinet member, the embassy reportedly described her to the [State Department](/wiki/State_Department "State Department") as a possible future prime minister. The description helped Thatcher to meet prominent people during a busy itinerary focused on economic issues, including [Paul Samuelson](/wiki/Paul_Samuelson "Paul Samuelson"), [Walt Rostow](/wiki/Walt_Rostow "Walt Rostow"), [Pierre-Paul Schweitzer](/wiki/Pierre-Paul_Schweitzer "Pierre-Paul Schweitzer") and [Nelson Rockefeller](/wiki/Nelson_Rockefeller "Nelson Rockefeller"). Following the visit, Heath appointed Thatcher to the Shadow Cabinet[67] as fuel and power spokeswoman.[76] Before the [1970 general election](/wiki/1970_United_Kingdom_general_election "1970 United Kingdom general election"), she was promoted to shadow transport spokeswoman and later to education.[77][78][79]

In 1968 [Enoch Powell](/wiki/Enoch_Powell "Enoch Powell") delivered his ["Rivers of Blood" speech](/wiki/Rivers_of_Blood_speech "Rivers of Blood speech") in which he strongly criticised [Commonwealth](/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations "Commonwealth of Nations") immigration to the United Kingdom and the then-proposed [Race Relations Bill](/wiki/Race_Relations_Act_1968 "Race Relations Act 1968"). When Heath telephoned Thatcher to inform her that he would sack Powell from the Shadow Cabinet, she recalled that she "really thought that it was better to let things cool down for the present rather than heighten the crisis". She believed that his main points about Commonwealth immigration were correct and that the selected quotations from his speech had been taken out of context.[80] In a 1991 interview for _[Today](/wiki/Today_\(UK_newspaper\) "Today \(UK newspaper\)")_ , Thatcher stated that she thought Powell had "made a valid argument, if in sometimes regrettable terms".[81]

Around this time, she gave her first Commons speech as a shadow transport minister and highlighted the need for investment in [British Rail](/wiki/British_Rail "British Rail"). She argued: "[I]f we build bigger and better roads, they would soon be saturated with more vehicles and we would be no nearer solving the problem."[82] Thatcher made her first visit to the [Soviet Union](/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union") in the summer of 1969 as the Opposition transport spokeswoman, and in October, delivered a speech celebrating her ten years in Parliament. In early 1970 she told _The Finchley Press_ that she would like to see a "reversal of the permissive society".[83]

### Education Secretary (1970–1974)

[](/wiki/File:Girls_at_Baldock_County_Council_School_in_Hertfordshire_enjoy_a_drink_of_milk_during_a_break_in_the_school_day_in_1944._D20552.jpg)Thatcher abolished free milk for children aged 7–11 (_pictured_) in 1971 as [her predecessor](/wiki/Edward_Short,_Baron_Glenamara "Edward Short, Baron Glenamara") had done for older children in 1968.

The Conservative Party, led by Edward Heath, won the 1970 general election, and Thatcher was appointed to the [Cabinet](/wiki/Cabinet_of_the_United_Kingdom "Cabinet of the United Kingdom") as [Secretary of State for Education and Science](/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Education_and_Science "Secretary of State for Education and Science"). Thatcher caused controversy when, after only a few days in office, she withdrew Labour's [Circular 10/65](/wiki/Circular_10/65 "Circular 10/65"), which attempted to force [comprehensivisation](/wiki/Comprehensive_school_\(England_and_Wales\) "Comprehensive school \(England and Wales\)"), without going through a consultation process. She was highly criticised for the speed at which she carried this out.[84] Consequently, she drafted her own new policy ([Circular 10/70](/wiki/Circular_10/70 "Circular 10/70")), which ensured that local authorities were not forced to go comprehensive. Her new policy was not meant to stop the development of new comprehensives; she said: "We shall [...] expect plans to be based on educational considerations rather than on the comprehensive principle."[85]

Thatcher supported [Lord Rothschild](/wiki/Victor_Rothschild,_3rd_Baron_Rothschild "Victor Rothschild, 3rd Baron Rothschild")'s 1971 proposal for market forces to affect government funding of research. Although many scientists opposed the proposal, her research background probably made her sceptical of their claim that outsiders should not interfere with funding.[32] The department evaluated proposals for more local education authorities to close grammar schools and to adopt [comprehensive secondary education](/wiki/Comprehensive_school "Comprehensive school"). Although Thatcher was committed to a tiered [secondary modern](/wiki/Secondary_modern "Secondary modern")-grammar school system of education and attempted to preserve grammar schools,[86] during her tenure as education secretary, she turned down only 326 of 3,612 proposals (roughly 9 per cent)[87] for schools to become comprehensives; the proportion of pupils attending comprehensive schools consequently rose from 32 per cent to 62 per cent.[88] Nevertheless, she managed to save 94 grammar schools.[85]

During her first months in office, she attracted public attention due to the government's attempts to cut spending. She gave priority to academic needs in schools,[86] while administering public expenditure cuts on the state education system, resulting in the abolition of [free milk for schoolchildren](/wiki/Education_Act_1944 "Education Act 1944") aged seven to eleven.[89] She held that few children would suffer if schools were charged for milk but agreed to provide younger children with 0.3 imperial pints (0.17 L) daily for nutritional purposes.[89] She also argued that she was simply carrying on with what the Labour government had started since they had stopped giving free milk to secondary schools.[90] Milk would still be provided to those children that required it on medical grounds, and schools could still sell milk.[90] The aftermath of the milk row hardened her determination; she told the editor-proprietor Harold Creighton of _[The Spectator](/wiki/The_Spectator "The Spectator")_ : "Don't underestimate me, I saw how they broke [Keith [Joseph]](/wiki/Keith_Joseph "Keith Joseph"), but they won't break me."[91]

Cabinet papers later revealed that she opposed the policy but had been forced into it by the Treasury.[92] Her decision provoked a storm of protest from Labour and the press,[93] leading to her being notoriously nicknamed "Margaret Thatcher, Milk Snatcher".[89][94] She reportedly considered leaving politics in the aftermath and later wrote in her autobiography: "I learned a valuable lesson. I had incurred the maximum of political odium for the minimum of political benefit."[95]

### Leader of the Opposition (1975–1979)

See also: [Thatcher shadow cabinet](/wiki/Thatcher_shadow_cabinet "Thatcher shadow cabinet")

External audio  
---  
1975 speech to the [National Press Club](/wiki/National_Press_Club_\(United_States\) "National Press Club \(United States\)")  
[](/wiki/File:Thatcher-loc.jpg "Thatcher sitting in a black-and-white photograph")Thatcher in late 1975  
[](/wiki/File:Nuvola_apps_arts.svg) [_National Press Club Luncheon Speakers: Margaret Thatcher_](http://www.loc.gov/rr/record/pressclub/thatcher.html) (Speech).[96] (Starts at 7:39, finishes at 28:33.)[97]  
  
The [Heath government](/wiki/Heath_government "Heath government") continued to experience difficulties with [oil embargoes](/wiki/1973_oil_crisis "1973 oil crisis") and union demands for wage increases in 1973, subsequently losing the [February 1974 general election](/wiki/February_1974_United_Kingdom_general_election "February 1974 United Kingdom general election").[93] Labour formed [a minority government](/wiki/Labour_government,_1974%E2%80%931979 "Labour government, 1974–1979") and went on to win a narrow majority in the [October 1974 general election](/wiki/October_1974_United_Kingdom_general_election "October 1974 United Kingdom general election"). Heath's leadership of the Conservative Party looked increasingly in doubt.[98] Thatcher was not initially seen as the obvious replacement, but she eventually became the main challenger, promising a fresh start.[99] Her main support came from the parliamentary [1922 Committee](/wiki/1922_Committee "1922 Committee")[99] and _The Spectator_ ,[100] but Thatcher's time in office gave her the reputation of a pragmatist rather than that of an ideologue.[25] She [defeated Heath](/wiki/1975_Conservative_Party_leadership_election "1975 Conservative Party leadership election") on the first ballot, and he resigned from the leadership.[101] In the second ballot she defeated Whitelaw, Heath's preferred successor. Thatcher's election had a polarising effect on the party; her support was stronger among MPs on the right, and also among those from southern England, and those who had not attended public schools or [Oxbridge](/wiki/Oxbridge "Oxbridge").[102]

Thatcher became Conservative Party leader and [Leader of the Opposition](/wiki/Leader_of_the_Opposition_\(United_Kingdom\) "Leader of the Opposition \(United Kingdom\)") on 11 February 1975;[103] she appointed Whitelaw as her [deputy](/wiki/Deputy_Leader_of_the_Conservative_Party_\(UK\) "Deputy Leader of the Conservative Party \(UK\)"). Heath was never reconciled to Thatcher's leadership of the party.[104]

The television critic [Clive James](/wiki/Clive_James "Clive James"), writing in _[The Observer](/wiki/The_Observer "The Observer")_ prior to her election as Conservative Party leader, compared her voice of 1973 to "a cat sliding down a blackboard".[nb 5] Thatcher had already begun to work on her presentation on the advice of [Gordon Reece](/wiki/Gordon_Reece "Gordon Reece"), a former television producer. By chance, Reece met the actor [Laurence Olivier](/wiki/Laurence_Olivier "Laurence Olivier"), who arranged lessons with the [National Theatre](/wiki/Royal_National_Theatre "Royal National Theatre")'s voice coach.[106][107][nb 6]

Thatcher began attending lunches regularly at the [Institute of Economic Affairs](/wiki/Institute_of_Economic_Affairs "Institute of Economic Affairs") (IEA), a think tank founded by the [Hayekian](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Hayekian#English "wikt:Hayekian") poultry magnate [Antony Fisher](/wiki/Antony_Fisher "Antony Fisher"); she had been visiting the IEA and reading its publications since the early 1960s. There she was influenced by the ideas of [Ralph Harris](/wiki/Ralph_Harris,_Baron_Harris_of_High_Cross "Ralph Harris, Baron Harris of High Cross") and [Arthur Seldon](/wiki/Arthur_Seldon "Arthur Seldon") and became the face of the ideological movement opposing the [British welfare state](/wiki/British_welfare_state "British welfare state"). [Keynesian economics](/wiki/Keynesian_economics "Keynesian economics"), they believed, was weakening Britain. The institute's pamphlets proposed less government, lower taxes, and more freedom for business and consumers.[110]

[](/wiki/File:President_Gerald_Ford_Meeting_with_Great_Britain%27s_Conservative_Party_Leader_Margaret_Thatcher_in_the_Oval_Office.jpg)With President [Gerald Ford](/wiki/Gerald_Ford "Gerald Ford") in the [Oval Office](/wiki/Oval_Office "Oval Office"), 1975

Thatcher intended to promote [neoliberal](/wiki/Neoliberal "Neoliberal") economic ideas at home and abroad. Despite setting the direction of her foreign policy for a Conservative government, Thatcher was distressed by her repeated failure to shine in the House of Commons. Consequently, Thatcher decided that as "her voice was carrying little weight at home", she would "be heard in the wider world".[111] Thatcher undertook visits across the Atlantic, establishing an international profile and promoting her economic and foreign policies. She toured the United States in 1975 and met President [Gerald Ford](/wiki/Gerald_Ford "Gerald Ford"),[112] visiting again in 1977, when she met President [Jimmy Carter](/wiki/Jimmy_Carter "Jimmy Carter").[113] Among other foreign trips, she met Shah [Mohammad Reza Pahlavi](/wiki/Mohammad_Reza_Pahlavi "Mohammad Reza Pahlavi") during a visit to [Iran](/wiki/Pahlavi_Iran "Pahlavi Iran") in 1978.[114] Thatcher chose to travel without being accompanied by her [shadow foreign secretary](/wiki/Shadow_foreign_secretary "Shadow foreign secretary"), [Reginald Maudling](/wiki/Reginald_Maudling "Reginald Maudling"), in an attempt to make a bolder personal impact.[113]

In domestic affairs, Thatcher opposed [Scottish devolution](/wiki/Scottish_devolution "Scottish devolution") ([home rule](/wiki/Home_rule#Scotland "Home rule")) and the creation of a [Scottish Assembly](/wiki/Scottish_Assembly "Scottish Assembly"). She instructed Conservative MPs to vote against the Scotland and Wales Bill in December 1976, which was successfully defeated, and then when new Bills were proposed, she supported amending the legislation to allow the English to vote in the [1979 referendum](/wiki/1979_Scottish_devolution_referendum "1979 Scottish devolution referendum") on Scottish devolution.[115]

Britain's economy during the 1970s was so weak that then Foreign Secretary [James Callaghan](/wiki/James_Callaghan "James Callaghan") warned his fellow Labour Cabinet members in 1974 of the possibility of "a breakdown of democracy", telling them: "If I were a young man, I would emigrate."[116] In mid-1978, the economy began to recover, and opinion polls showed Labour in the lead, with a general election being expected later that year and a Labour win a serious possibility. Now prime minister, Callaghan surprised many by announcing on 7 September that there would be no general election that year and that he would wait until 1979 before going to the polls. Thatcher reacted to this by branding the Labour government "chickens", and Liberal Party leader David Steel joined in, criticising Labour for "running scared".[117]

The Labour government then faced fresh public unease about the direction of the country and a damaging series of strikes during the winter of 1978–79, dubbed the "[Winter of Discontent](/wiki/Winter_of_Discontent "Winter of Discontent")". The Conservatives attacked the Labour government's unemployment record, using advertising with the slogan "[Labour Isn't Working](/wiki/Labour_Isn%27t_Working "Labour Isn't Working")". A [general election](/wiki/1979_United_Kingdom_general_election "1979 United Kingdom general election") was called after the Callaghan ministry [lost a motion of no confidence](/wiki/1979_vote_of_no_confidence_in_the_Callaghan_ministry "1979 vote of no confidence in the Callaghan ministry") in early 1979. The Conservatives won a 44-seat majority in the House of Commons, and Thatcher became the first female British prime minister.[118]

#### "Iron Lady"

Main article: [Britain Awake](/wiki/Britain_Awake "Britain Awake")

External videos  
---  
1976 speech to Finchley Conservatives  
[](/wiki/File:Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg) [_Speech to Finchley Conservatives_ (admits to being an "Iron Lady")__](https://www.margaretthatcher.org/document/111324)(Speech)- via the Margaret Thatcher Foundation.[119]  
  
> I stand before you tonight in my _Red Star_ chiffon evening gown, my face softly made up and my fair hair gently waved, the Iron Lady of the Western world.[119]

— Thatcher embracing her Soviet nickname in 1976

In 1976, Thatcher gave her "Britain Awake" foreign policy speech which lambasted the Soviet Union, saying it was "bent on world dominance".[120] The Soviet Army journal _[Red Star](/wiki/Krasnaya_Zvezda "Krasnaya Zvezda")_ reported her stance in a piece headlined "Iron Lady Raises Fears",[121] alluding to her remarks on the [Iron Curtain](/wiki/Iron_Curtain "Iron Curtain").[120] _[The Sunday Times](/wiki/The_Sunday_Times "The Sunday Times")_ covered the _Red Star_ article the next day,[122] and Thatcher embraced the [epithet](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/epithet "wikt:epithet") a week later; in a speech to Finchley Conservatives she likened it to [the Duke of Wellington](/wiki/The_Duke_of_Wellington "The Duke of Wellington")'s nickname "Iron Duke".[119] The ["Iron" metaphor](/wiki/Iron_\(metaphor\) "Iron \(metaphor\)") followed her throughout the rest of her career,[123] and would become a generic [sobriquet](/wiki/Sobriquet "Sobriquet") for other strong-willed female politicians.[124]

## Prime Minister of the United Kingdom (1979–1990)

Main article: [Premiership of Margaret Thatcher](/wiki/Premiership_of_Margaret_Thatcher "Premiership of Margaret Thatcher")

Further information: [First Thatcher ministry](/wiki/First_Thatcher_ministry "First Thatcher ministry"), [second Thatcher ministry](/wiki/Second_Thatcher_ministry "Second Thatcher ministry"), and [third Thatcher ministry](/wiki/Third_Thatcher_ministry "Third Thatcher ministry")

External videos  
---  
1979 remarks on becoming prime minister  
[](/wiki/File:10_Downing_Street_235.jpg)[10 Downing Street](/wiki/10_Downing_Street "10 Downing Street"), c. 1979  
[](/wiki/File:Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg) [_Remarks on becoming Prime Minister_ (St Francis's prayer)__](https://www.margaretthatcher.org/document/115355)(Speech)- via the Margaret Thatcher Foundation.[125]  
  
Thatcher became prime minister on 4 May 1979. Arriving at [Downing Street](/wiki/Downing_Street "Downing Street") she said, paraphrasing the [Prayer of Saint Francis](/wiki/Prayer_of_Saint_Francis "Prayer of Saint Francis"): 

> Where there is discord, may we bring harmony;  
>  Where there is error, may we bring truth;  
>  Where there is doubt, may we bring faith;  
>  And where there is despair, may we bring hope.[125]

In office throughout the 1980s, Thatcher was frequently referred to as the most powerful woman in the world.[126][127][128]

### Domestic affairs

#### Minorities

Thatcher was the Opposition leader and prime minister at a time of increased racial tension in Britain. During the [1977 local elections](/wiki/1977_United_Kingdom_local_elections "1977 United Kingdom local elections"), _[The Economist](/wiki/The_Economist "The Economist")_ commented: "The Tory tide swamped the smaller parties - specifically the [National Front [NF]](/wiki/National_Front_\(UK\) "National Front \(UK\)"), which suffered a clear decline from last year."[129][130] Her standing in the polls had risen by 11% after a 1978 interview for _[World in Action](/wiki/World_in_Action "World in Action")_ in which she said "the British character has done so much for democracy, for law and done so much throughout the world that if there is any fear that it might be swamped people are going to react and be rather hostile to those coming in", as well as "in many ways [minorities] add to the richness and variety of this country. The moment the minority threatens to become a big one, people get frightened".[131][132] In the 1979 general election, the Conservatives had attracted votes from the NF, whose support almost collapsed.[133] In a July 1979 meeting with Foreign Secretary [Lord Carrington](/wiki/Lord_Carrington "Lord Carrington") and Home Secretary William Whitelaw, Thatcher objected to the number of Asian immigrants, in the context of limiting the total of [Vietnamese boat people](/wiki/Vietnamese_boat_people "Vietnamese boat people") allowed to settle in the UK to fewer than 10,000 over two years.[134]

#### The Queen

As prime minister, Thatcher met [Queen Elizabeth II](/wiki/Queen_Elizabeth_II "Queen Elizabeth II") weekly to discuss government business, and their relationship came under scrutiny.[135] Campbell (2011a, p. 464) states: 

> One question that continued to fascinate the public about the phenomenon of a woman Prime Minister was how she got on with the Queen. The answer is that their relations were punctiliously correct, but there was little love lost on either side. As two women of very similar age – Mrs Thatcher was six months older – occupying parallel positions at the top of the social pyramid, one the head of government, the other head of state, they were bound to be in some sense rivals. Mrs Thatcher's attitude to the Queen was ambivalent. On the one hand she had an almost mystical reverence for the institution of the monarchy [...] Yet at the same time she was trying to modernise the country and sweep away many of the values and practices which the monarchy perpetuated.

[Michael Shea](/wiki/Michael_Shea_\(diplomat\) "Michael Shea \(diplomat\)"), the Queen's press secretary, in 1986 leaked stories of a deep rift to _The Sunday Times_. He said that she felt Thatcher's policies were "uncaring, confrontational and socially divisive".[136] Thatcher later wrote: "I always found the Queen's attitude towards the work of the Government absolutely correct [...] stories of clashes between 'two powerful women' were just too good not to make up."[137]

#### Economy and taxation

See also: [1979 budget](/wiki/June_1979_United_Kingdom_budget "June 1979 United Kingdom budget")

Economic growth and public spending
