Housing  | −67.0   
Source: Stewart (2013, Appendix) 

Thatcher's economic policy was influenced by [monetarist](/wiki/Monetarist "Monetarist") thinking and economists such as [Milton Friedman](/wiki/Milton_Friedman "Milton Friedman") and [Alan Walters](/wiki/Alan_Walters "Alan Walters").[138] Together with her first [chancellor](/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_Exchequer "Chancellor of the Exchequer"), [Geoffrey Howe](/wiki/Geoffrey_Howe "Geoffrey Howe"), she lowered direct taxes on income and increased indirect taxes.[139] She increased interest rates to slow the growth of the money supply, and thereby lower inflation;[138] introduced cash limits on public spending and reduced expenditure on social services such as education and housing.[139] Cuts to higher education led to Thatcher being the first [Oxonian](/wiki/Oxonian "Oxonian") post-war prime minister without an honorary doctorate from Oxford University after a 738–319 vote of the governing assembly and a student petition.[140]

Some Heathite Conservatives in the Cabinet, the so-called "[wets](/wiki/Wets "Wets")", expressed doubt over Thatcher's policies.[141] The [1981 England riots](/wiki/1981_England_riots "1981 England riots") resulted in the British media discussing the need for a [policy U-turn](/wiki/Flip-flop_\(politics\) "Flip-flop \(politics\)"). At the 1980 Conservative Party conference, Thatcher addressed the issue directly with a speech written by the playwright [Ronald Millar](/wiki/Ronald_Millar "Ronald Millar"),[142] that notably included the following lines:

> To those waiting with bated breath for that favourite media catchphrase, the "U" turn, I have only one thing to say. "You turn if you want to. [The lady's not for turning](/wiki/The_lady%27s_not_for_turning "The lady's not for turning")."[143]

See also: [1981 budget](/wiki/1981_United_Kingdom_budget "1981 United Kingdom budget")

Thatcher's job approval rating fell to 23% by December 1980, lower than recorded for any previous prime minister.[144] As the [recession of the early 1980s](/wiki/Early_1980s_recession "Early 1980s recession") deepened, she increased taxes,[145] despite concerns expressed in a March 1981 statement signed by 364 leading economists,[146] which argued there was "no basis in economic theory [...] for the Government's belief that by deflating demand they will bring inflation permanently under control", adding that "present policies will deepen the depression, erode the industrial base of our economy and threaten its social and political stability".[147]

[](/wiki/File:Margaret_Thatcher_visiting_Salford.jpg)Visiting [Salford University](/wiki/Salford_University "Salford University") in 1982

By 1982 the UK began to experience signs of economic recovery;[148] inflation was down to 8.6% from a high of 18%, but unemployment was over 3 million for the first time since the 1930s.[149] By 1983 overall economic growth was stronger, and inflation and mortgage rates had fallen to their lowest levels in 13 years, although manufacturing employment as a share of total employment fell to just over 30%,[150] with total unemployment remaining high, peaking at 3.3 million in 1984.[151]

During the 1982 Conservative Party Conference, Thatcher said: "We have done more to roll back the frontiers of socialism than any previous Conservative Government."[152] She said at the Party Conference the following year that the British people had completely rejected [state socialism](/wiki/State_socialism "State socialism") and understood "the state has no source of money other than money which people earn themselves [...] There is no such thing as public money; there is only taxpayers' money."[153]

By 1987 unemployment was falling, the economy was stable and strong, and inflation was low. Opinion polls showed a comfortable Conservative lead, and [local council election](/wiki/1987_United_Kingdom_local_elections "1987 United Kingdom local elections") results had also been successful, prompting Thatcher to call a general election for 11 June that year, despite the deadline for an election still being 12 months away. The [election](/wiki/1987_United_Kingdom_general_election "1987 United Kingdom general election") saw Thatcher re-elected for a third successive term.[154]

Thatcher had been firmly opposed to British membership of the [Exchange Rate Mechanism](/wiki/Exchange_Rate_Mechanism "Exchange Rate Mechanism") (ERM, a precursor to [European Economic and Monetary Union](/wiki/European_Economic_and_Monetary_Union "European Economic and Monetary Union")), believing that it would constrain the British economy,[155] despite the urging of both Chancellor of the Exchequer [Nigel Lawson](/wiki/Nigel_Lawson "Nigel Lawson") and Foreign Secretary Geoffrey Howe;[156] in October 1990 she was persuaded by [John Major](/wiki/John_Major "John Major"), Lawson's successor as chancellor, to join the ERM at what proved to be too high a rate.[157]

Thatcher reformed local government taxes by replacing [domestic rates](/wiki/Rates_\(tax\) "Rates \(tax\)") (a tax based on the nominal rental value of a home) with the [Community Charge](/wiki/Community_Charge "Community Charge") (or poll tax) in which the same amount was charged to each adult resident.[158] The new tax was introduced in Scotland in 1989 and in England and Wales the following year,[159] and proved to be among the most unpopular policies of her premiership.[158] Public disquiet culminated in a 70,000 to 200,000-strong[160] demonstration in London in March 1990; the demonstration around [Trafalgar Square](/wiki/Trafalgar_Square "Trafalgar Square") deteriorated into [riots](/wiki/Poll_tax_riots "Poll tax riots"), leaving 113 people injured and 340 under arrest.[161] The Community Charge was abolished in 1991 by her successor, John Major.[161] It has since transpired that Thatcher herself had failed to register for the tax and was threatened with financial penalties if she did not return her form.[162]

#### Industrial relations

See also: [GCHQ trade union ban](/wiki/GCHQ_trade_union_ban "GCHQ trade union ban") and [the GCHQ case](/wiki/Council_of_Civil_Service_Unions_v_Minister_for_the_Civil_Service "Council of Civil Service Unions v Minister for the Civil Service")

Thatcher believed that the [trade unions](/wiki/Trade_unions_in_the_United_Kingdom "Trade unions in the United Kingdom") were harmful to both ordinary trade unionists and the public.[163] She was committed to reducing the power of the unions, whose leadership she accused of undermining parliamentary democracy and economic performance through strike action.[164] Several unions launched strikes in response to legislation introduced to limit their power, but resistance eventually collapsed.[165] Only 39% of union members voted Labour in the 1983 general election.[166] According to the BBC's political correspondent in 2004, Thatcher "managed to destroy the power of the trade unions for almost a generation".[167] The [miners' strike of 1984–85](/wiki/Miners%27_strike_of_1984%E2%80%9385 "Miners' strike of 1984–85") was the biggest and most devastating confrontation between the unions and the Thatcher government.[168]

[](/wiki/File:Miners_strike_rally_London_1984.jpg)Pro-strike rally in London, 1984

In March 1984 the [National Coal Board](/wiki/National_Coal_Board "National Coal Board") (NCB) proposed to close 20 of the 174 state-owned mines and cut 20,000 jobs out of 187,000.[169][170][171] Two-thirds of the country's miners, led by the [National Union of Mineworkers](/wiki/National_Union_of_Mineworkers_\(Great_Britain\) "National Union of Mineworkers \(Great Britain\)") (NUM) under [Arthur Scargill](/wiki/Arthur_Scargill "Arthur Scargill"), went on strike in protest.[169][172][173] However, Scargill refused to hold a ballot on the strike,[174] having previously lost three ballots on a national strike (in January and October 1982, and March 1983).[175] This led to the strike being declared illegal by the [High Court of Justice](/wiki/High_Court_of_Justice "High Court of Justice").[176][177]

Thatcher refused to meet the union's demands and compared the miners' dispute to the [Falklands War](/wiki/Falklands_War "Falklands War"), declaring in a speech in 1984: "We had to fight the enemy without in the Falklands. We always have to be aware of the enemy within, which is much more difficult to fight and more dangerous to liberty."[178] Thatcher's opponents characterised her words as indicating contempt for the working class and have been employed in criticism of her ever since.[179]

After a year out on strike in March 1985, the NUM leadership conceded without a deal. The cost to the economy was estimated to be at least £1.5 billion, and the strike was blamed for much of the [pound](/wiki/Pound_sterling "Pound sterling")'s fall against the US dollar.[180] Thatcher reflected on the end of the strike in her statement that "if anyone has won", it was "the miners who stayed at work" and all those "that have kept Britain going".[181]

The government closed 25 unprofitable coal mines in 1985, and by 1992 a total of 97 mines had been closed;[171] those that remained were privatised in 1994.[182] The resulting closure of 150 coal mines, some of which were not losing money, resulted in the loss of tens of thousands of jobs and had the effect of devastating entire communities.[171] Strikes had helped bring down Heath's government, and Thatcher was determined to succeed where he had failed. Her strategy of preparing fuel stocks, appointing hardliner [Ian MacGregor](/wiki/Ian_MacGregor "Ian MacGregor") as NCB leader and ensuring that police were adequately trained and equipped with riot gear contributed to her triumph over the striking miners.[183]

The number of stoppages across the UK peaked at 4,583 in 1979, when more than 29 million working days had been lost. In 1984, the year of the miners' strike, there were 1,221, resulting in the loss of more than 27 million working days. Stoppages then fell steadily throughout the rest of Thatcher's premiership; in 1990, there were 630 and fewer than 2 million working days lost, and they continued to fall thereafter.[184] Thatcher's tenure also witnessed a sharp decline in trade union density, with the percentage of workers belonging to a trade union falling from 57.3% in 1979 to 49.5% in 1985.[185] In 1979 up until Thatcher's final year in office, trade union membership also fell, from 13.5 million in 1979 to fewer than 10 million.[186]

#### Privatisation

The policy of [privatisation](/wiki/Privatisation "Privatisation") has been called "a crucial ingredient of Thatcherism".[187] After the 1983 election, the sale of state utilities accelerated;[188] more than £29 billion was raised from the sale of nationalised industries, and another £18 billion from the sale of council houses.[189] The process of privatisation, especially the preparation of nationalised industries for privatisation, was associated with marked improvements in performance, particularly in terms of [labour productivity](/wiki/Labour_productivity "Labour productivity").[190]

Some of the privatised industries, including gas, [water](/wiki/Water_privatisation_in_England_and_Wales "Water privatisation in England and Wales") and electricity, were [natural monopolies](/wiki/Natural_monopolies "Natural monopolies") for which privatisation involved little increase in competition. The privatised industries that demonstrated improvement sometimes did so while still under state ownership. [British Steel Corporation](/wiki/British_Steel_Corporation "British Steel Corporation") had made great gains in profitability while still a nationalised industry under the government-appointed MacGregor chairmanship, which faced down trade-union opposition to close plants and halve the workforce.[191] Regulation was also significantly expanded to compensate for the loss of direct government control, with the foundation of regulatory bodies such as [Oftel](/wiki/Oftel "Oftel") ([1984](/wiki/Telecommunications_Act_1984 "Telecommunications Act 1984")), [Ofgas](/wiki/Ofgas "Ofgas") ([1986](/wiki/Gas_Act_1986 "Gas Act 1986")), and the [National Rivers Authority](/wiki/National_Rivers_Authority "National Rivers Authority") ([1989](/wiki/Water_Act_1989 "Water Act 1989")).[192] There was no clear pattern to the degree of competition, regulation, and performance among the privatised industries.[190]

In most cases, privatisation benefited consumers in terms of lower prices and improved efficiency but results overall have been mixed.[193] Not all privatised companies have had successful share price trajectories in the longer term.[194] A 2010 review by the free-market think tank the Institute of Economic Affairs states: "[I]t does seem to be the case that once competition and/or effective regulation was introduced, performance improved markedly [...] But I hasten to emphasise again that the literature is not unanimous."[195]

Thatcher always resisted [privatising British Rail](/wiki/Privatisation_of_British_Rail "Privatisation of British Rail") and was said to have told Transport Secretary [Nicholas Ridley](/wiki/Nicholas_Ridley,_Baron_Ridley_of_Liddesdale "Nicholas Ridley, Baron Ridley of Liddesdale"): "Railway privatisation will be the [Waterloo](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Waterloo#Noun "wikt:Waterloo") of this government. Please never mention the railways to me again." Shortly before her resignation in 1990, she accepted the arguments for privatisation, which her successor John Major implemented in 1994.[196]

The privatisation of public assets was combined with [financial deregulation](/wiki/Deregulation#United_Kingdom "Deregulation") to fuel economic growth. Chancellor Geoffrey Howe abolished the UK's exchange controls in 1979,[197] which allowed more capital to be invested in foreign markets, and the [Big Bang](/wiki/Big_Bang_\(financial_markets\) "Big Bang \(financial markets\)") of 1986 removed many restrictions on the [London Stock Exchange](/wiki/London_Stock_Exchange "London Stock Exchange").[197]

#### Northern Ireland

[](/wiki/File:Margaret_Thatcher_on_a_visit_to_Northern_Ireland.jpg)Visiting Northern Ireland in 1982

In 1980 and 1981, [Provisional Irish Republican Army](/wiki/Provisional_Irish_Republican_Army "Provisional Irish Republican Army") (PIRA) and [Irish National Liberation Army](/wiki/Irish_National_Liberation_Army "Irish National Liberation Army") (INLA) prisoners in Northern Ireland's [Maze Prison](/wiki/Maze_Prison "Maze Prison") carried out [hunger strikes](/wiki/1981_Irish_hunger_strike "1981 Irish hunger strike") to regain the status of political prisoners that had been removed in 1976 by the preceding Labour government.[198] [Bobby Sands](/wiki/Bobby_Sands "Bobby Sands") began the 1981 strike, saying that he would fast until death unless prison inmates won concessions over their living conditions.[198] Thatcher refused to countenance a return to political status for the prisoners, having declared "Crime is crime is crime; it is not political".[198] Nevertheless, the British government privately contacted republican leaders in a bid to bring the hunger strikes to an end.[199] After the deaths of Sands and nine others, the strike ended. Some rights were restored to paramilitary prisoners, but not official recognition of political status.[200] Violence in Northern Ireland escalated significantly during the hunger strikes.[201]

Thatcher narrowly escaped injury in an IRA [assassination attempt](/wiki/Brighton_hotel_bombing "Brighton hotel bombing") at a Brighton hotel early in the morning on 12 October 1984.[202] Five people were killed, including the wife of minister [John Wakeham](/wiki/John_Wakeham "John Wakeham"). Thatcher was staying at the hotel to prepare for the Conservative Party conference, which she insisted should open as scheduled the following day.[202] She delivered her speech as planned,[203] though rewritten from her original draft,[204] in a move that was supported across the political spectrum and enhanced her popularity with the public.[205]

On 6 November 1981, Thatcher and [Taoiseach](/wiki/Taoiseach "Taoiseach") (Irish prime minister) [Garret FitzGerald](/wiki/Garret_FitzGerald "Garret FitzGerald") had established the Anglo-Irish Inter-Governmental Council, a forum for meetings between the two governments.[200] On 15 November 1985, Thatcher and FitzGerald signed the Hillsborough [Anglo-Irish Agreement](/wiki/Anglo-Irish_Agreement "Anglo-Irish Agreement"), which marked the first time a British government had given the Republic of Ireland an advisory role in the governance of Northern Ireland. In protest, the [Ulster Says No](/wiki/Ulster_Says_No "Ulster Says No") movement led by [Ian Paisley](/wiki/Ian_Paisley "Ian Paisley") attracted 100,000 to a rally in Belfast,[206] [Ian Gow](/wiki/Ian_Gow "Ian Gow"), later assassinated by the PIRA, resigned as [Minister of State](/wiki/Minister_of_State "Minister of State") in [HM Treasury](/wiki/HM_Treasury "HM Treasury"),[207][208] and all 15 Unionist MPs resigned their parliamentary seats; only one was not returned in the subsequent [by-elections](/wiki/List_of_United_Kingdom_by-elections_\(1979%E2%80%932010\) "List of United Kingdom by-elections \(1979–2010\)") on 23 January 1986.[209]

### Environment

Thatcher supported an active [climate protection](/wiki/Climate_protection "Climate protection") policy; she was instrumental in the passing of the [Environmental Protection Act 1990](/wiki/Environmental_Protection_Act_1990 "Environmental Protection Act 1990"),[210] the founding of the [Hadley Centre for Climate Research and Prediction](/wiki/Hadley_Centre_for_Climate_Research_and_Prediction "Hadley Centre for Climate Research and Prediction"),[211] the establishment of the [Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change](/wiki/Intergovernmental_Panel_on_Climate_Change "Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change"),[212] and the ratification of the [Montreal Protocol](/wiki/Montreal_Protocol "Montreal Protocol") on preserving the [ozone](/wiki/Ozone_layer "Ozone layer").[213]

Thatcher helped to put [climate change](/wiki/Climate_change "Climate change"), [acid rain](/wiki/Acid_rain "Acid rain") and general pollution in the British mainstream in the late 1980s,[212][214] calling for a global treaty on climate change in 1989.[215] Her speeches included one to the [Royal Society](/wiki/Royal_Society "Royal Society") in 1988,[216] followed by another to the [UN General Assembly](/wiki/UN_General_Assembly "UN General Assembly") in 1989.[217] However, by the 2003 release of her book _[Statecraft](/wiki/Statecraft_\(book\) "Statecraft \(book\)")_ , she had revised her opinion on climate change.[212]

### Foreign affairs

[](/wiki/File:Jimmy_Carter_and_Margaret_Thatcher_in_1979_\(cropped\).jpg)

With President [Jimmy Carter](/wiki/Jimmy_Carter "Jimmy Carter") in the Oval Office, 1979

[](/wiki/File:President_Ronald_Reagan_and_Prime_Minister_Margaret_Thatcher_of_the_United_Kingdom.jpg)

With President [Ronald Reagan](/wiki/Ronald_Reagan "Ronald Reagan") in the Oval Office, 1988

[](/wiki/File:Thatcher_and_Bush_-_1990_-_P14935-18A.jpg)

With President [George H. W. Bush](/wiki/George_H._W._Bush "George H. W. Bush") in 1990

Thatcher appointed Lord Carrington, an ennobled member of the party and former [Secretary of State for Defence](/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Defence "Secretary of State for Defence"), to run the [Foreign Office](/wiki/Foreign_Office "Foreign Office") in 1979.[218] Although considered a "wet", he avoided domestic affairs and got along well with Thatcher. One issue was what to do with [Rhodesia](/wiki/Rhodesia "Rhodesia"), where the white minority had determined to rule the prosperous, black-majority breakaway colony in the face of overwhelming international criticism. With the 1975 [Portuguese](/wiki/Portuguese_Empire "Portuguese Empire") collapse in the continent, South Africa (which had been Rhodesia's chief supporter) realised that their ally was a liability; black rule was inevitable, and the Thatcher government brokered a peaceful solution to end the [Rhodesian Bush War](/wiki/Rhodesian_Bush_War "Rhodesian Bush War") in December 1979 via the [Lancaster House Agreement](/wiki/Lancaster_House_Agreement "Lancaster House Agreement"). The conference at Lancaster House was attended by Rhodesian prime minister [Ian Smith](/wiki/Ian_Smith "Ian Smith"), as well as by the key black leaders: [Muzorewa](/wiki/Muzorewa "Muzorewa"), [Mugabe](/wiki/Mugabe "Mugabe"), [Nkomo](/wiki/Joshua_Nkomo "Joshua Nkomo") and [Tongogara](/wiki/Tongogara "Tongogara"). The result was the new Zimbabwean nation under black rule in 1980.[219]

#### Cold War

Thatcher's first foreign-policy crisis came with the 1979 [Soviet invasion of Afghanistan](/wiki/Soviet_invasion_of_Afghanistan "Soviet invasion of Afghanistan"). She condemned the invasion, said it showed the bankruptcy of a [détente](/wiki/D%C3%A9tente "Détente") policy and helped convince some British athletes to boycott the [1980 Moscow Olympics](/wiki/1980_Moscow_Olympics "1980 Moscow Olympics"). She gave weak support to US president Jimmy Carter who tried to punish the USSR with economic sanctions. Britain's economic situation was precarious, and most of NATO was reluctant to cut trade ties.[220] Thatcher nevertheless gave the go-ahead for [Whitehall](/wiki/Whitehall "Whitehall") to approve [MI6](/wiki/MI6 "MI6") (along with the SAS) to undertake ["disruptive action" in Afghanistan](/wiki/United_Kingdom_in_the_Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War "United Kingdom in the Soviet–Afghan War").[221] As well as working with the CIA in [Operation Cyclone](/wiki/Operation_Cyclone "Operation Cyclone"), they also supplied weapons, training and intelligence to the _[mujaheddin](/wiki/Mujaheddin "Mujaheddin")_.[222]

The _[Financial Times](/wiki/Financial_Times "Financial Times")_ reported in 2011 that her government had secretly supplied [Iraq under Saddam Hussein](/wiki/Iraq_under_Saddam_Hussein "Iraq under Saddam Hussein") with ["non-lethal" military equipment since 1981](/wiki/British_support_for_Iraq_during_the_Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_war "British support for Iraq during the Iran–Iraq war").[223][224]

Having withdrawn formal recognition from the [Pol Pot regime](/wiki/Pol_Pot_regime "Pol Pot regime") in 1979,[225] the Thatcher government backed the [Khmer Rouge](/wiki/Khmer_Rouge "Khmer Rouge") keeping their UN seat after they were ousted from power in Cambodia by the [Cambodian–Vietnamese War](/wiki/Cambodian%E2%80%93Vietnamese_War "Cambodian–Vietnamese War"). Although Thatcher denied it at the time,[226] it was revealed in 1991 that, while not directly training any Khmer Rouge,[227] from 1983 the [Special Air Service](/wiki/Special_Air_Service "Special Air Service") (SAS) was sent to secretly train "the armed forces of the [Cambodian non-communist resistance](/wiki/Coalition_Government_of_Democratic_Kampuchea "Coalition Government of Democratic Kampuchea")" that remained loyal to Prince [Norodom Sihanouk](/wiki/Norodom_Sihanouk "Norodom Sihanouk") and his former prime minister [Son Sann](/wiki/Son_Sann "Son Sann") in the fight against the [Vietnamese-backed puppet regime](/wiki/People%27s_Republic_of_Kampuchea "People's Republic of Kampuchea").[228][229]

Thatcher was one of the first Western leaders to respond warmly to the reformist Soviet leader [Mikhail Gorbachev](/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev "Mikhail Gorbachev"). Following Reagan–Gorbachev summit meetings and reforms enacted by Gorbachev in the USSR, she declared in November 1988 that "[w]e're not in a Cold War now" but rather in a "new relationship much wider than the Cold War ever was".[230] She went on a state visit to the Soviet Union in 1984 and met Gorbachev and Council of Ministers chairman [Nikolai Ryzhkov](/wiki/Nikolai_Ryzhkov "Nikolai Ryzhkov").[231]

#### Ties with the US

[](/wiki/File:Reagan-Thatcher_cabinet_talks.jpg)Meeting [Reagan's cabinet](/wiki/Reagan%27s_cabinet "Reagan's cabinet") with ministers in the [White House Cabinet Room](/wiki/White_House_Cabinet_Room "White House Cabinet Room"), 1981

Despite opposite personalities, Thatcher bonded quickly with US president [Ronald Reagan](/wiki/Ronald_Reagan "Ronald Reagan").[nb 7] She gave strong support to the [Reagan administration](/wiki/Reagan_administration "Reagan administration")'s [Cold War policies](/wiki/Reagan_Doctrine "Reagan Doctrine") based on their shared [distrust of communism](/wiki/Anti-communism "Anti-communism").[165] A sharp disagreement came in 1983 when Reagan did not consult with her on the [invasion of Grenada](/wiki/Invasion_of_Grenada "Invasion of Grenada").[232][233]

During her first year as prime minister, she supported [NATO](/wiki/NATO "NATO")'s decision to deploy US nuclear [cruise](/wiki/BGM-109G_Ground_Launched_Cruise_Missile "BGM-109G Ground Launched Cruise Missile") and [Pershing II](/wiki/Pershing_II "Pershing II") missiles in Western Europe,[165] permitting the US to station more than 160 cruise missiles at [RAF Greenham Common](/wiki/RAF_Greenham_Common "RAF Greenham Common"), starting in November 1983 and triggering mass protests by the [Campaign for Nuclear Disarmament](/wiki/Campaign_for_Nuclear_Disarmament "Campaign for Nuclear Disarmament").[165] She bought the [Trident nuclear missile](/wiki/Trident_nuclear_missile "Trident nuclear missile") submarine system from the US to replace Polaris, tripling the UK's nuclear forces[234] at an eventual cost of more than £12 billion (at 1996–97 prices).[235] Thatcher's preference for defence ties with the US was demonstrated in the [Westland affair](/wiki/Westland_affair "Westland affair") of 1985–86 when she acted with colleagues to allow the struggling helicopter manufacturer [Westland](/wiki/Westland_Helicopters "Westland Helicopters") to refuse a takeover offer from the Italian firm [Agusta](/wiki/Agusta "Agusta") in favour of the management's preferred option, a link with [Sikorsky Aircraft](/wiki/Sikorsky_Aircraft "Sikorsky Aircraft"). Defence Secretary [Michael Heseltine](/wiki/Michael_Heseltine "Michael Heseltine"), who had supported the Agusta deal, resigned from the government in protest.[236]

In April 1986 she permitted US [F-111s](/wiki/General_Dynamics_F-111_Aardvark "General Dynamics F-111 Aardvark") to use [Royal Air Force](/wiki/Royal_Air_Force "Royal Air Force") bases for the [bombing of Libya](/wiki/1986_United_States_bombing_of_Libya "1986 United States bombing of Libya") in retaliation for the [Libyan bombing of a Berlin discothèque](/wiki/West_Berlin_discotheque_bombing "West Berlin discotheque bombing"),[237] citing the right of self-defence under [Article 51 of the UN Charter](/wiki/Chapter_VII_of_the_United_Nations_Charter "Chapter VII of the United Nations Charter").[238][nb 8] Polls suggested that fewer than one in three British citizens approved of her decision.[240]

Thatcher was in the US on a state visit when Iraqi leader Saddam Hussein [invaded Kuwait](/wiki/Iraqi_invasion_of_Kuwait "Iraqi invasion of Kuwait") in August 1990.[241] During her talks with President [George H. W. Bush](/wiki/George_H._W._Bush "George H. W. Bush"), who succeeded Reagan in 1989, she recommended intervention,[241] and put pressure on Bush to deploy troops in the Middle East to drive the [Iraqi Army](/wiki/Iraqi_Army "Iraqi Army") out of Kuwait.[242] Bush was apprehensive about the plan, prompting Thatcher to remark to him during a telephone conversation: "This was no time to go wobbly!"[243][244] Thatcher's government supplied military forces to the international coalition in the build-up to the [Gulf War](/wiki/Gulf_War "Gulf War"), but she had resigned by the time hostilities began on 17 January 1991.[245][246] She applauded the coalition victory on the backbenches, while warning that "the victories of peace will take longer than the battles of war".[247] It was disclosed in 2017 that Thatcher had suggested threatening Saddam with [chemical weapons](/wiki/Chemical_weapon "Chemical weapon") after the invasion of Kuwait.[248][249]

#### Crisis in the South Atlantic

See also: ["Rejoice"](/wiki/Rejoice_\(Margaret_Thatcher\) "Rejoice \(Margaret Thatcher\)"), [sinking of the ARA _General Belgrano_](/wiki/Sinking_of_the_ARA_General_Belgrano "Sinking of the ARA General Belgrano"), and [the Diana Gould exchange](/wiki/Diana_Gould%E2%80%93Margaret_Thatcher_exchange "Diana Gould–Margaret Thatcher exchange")

On 2 April 1982, the [military junta in Argentina](/wiki/National_Reorganization_Process "National Reorganization Process") ordered the invasion of the [Crown colony](/wiki/Crown_colony "Crown colony") of the [Falkland Islands](/wiki/Falkland_Islands "Falkland Islands") and its dependency of [South Georgia](/wiki/South_Georgia "South Georgia"), [beginning the Falklands War](/wiki/Events_leading_to_the_Falklands_War "Events leading to the Falklands War").[250] The subsequent crisis was "a defining moment of [Thatcher's] premiership".[251] At the suggestion of Harold Macmillan and [Robert Armstrong](/wiki/Robert_Armstrong,_Baron_Armstrong_of_Ilminster "Robert Armstrong, Baron Armstrong of Ilminster"),[251] she set up and chaired a small [War Cabinet](/wiki/War_cabinet#Falklands_War "War cabinet") to oversee the conduct of the war,[252] which by 5–6 April had authorised and dispatched [a naval task force](/wiki/British_naval_forces_in_the_Falklands_War "British naval forces in the Falklands War") to retake the islands.[253] [Argentina surrendered](/wiki/Argentine_surrender_in_the_Falklands_War "Argentine surrender in the Falklands War") on 14 June and _Operation Corporate_ was hailed a success, notwithstanding the deaths of 255 British servicemen and three Falkland Islanders. Argentine fatalities totalled 649, half of them aboard [ARA  _General Belgrano_](/wiki/ARA_General_Belgrano "ARA General Belgrano") that was torpedoed and sunk by [HMS  _Conqueror_](/wiki/HMS_Conqueror_\(S48\) "HMS Conqueror \(S48\)") on 2 May.[254]

Thatcher was criticised for the neglect of the Falklands' defence that led to the war, and especially by Labour MP [Tam Dalyell](/wiki/Tam_Dalyell "Tam Dalyell") in Parliament for the decision to torpedo the _General Belgrano_ , but overall, she was considered a competent and committed war leader.[255] The "[Falklands factor](/wiki/Falklands_factor "Falklands factor")", an economic recovery beginning early in 1982, and a bitterly divided opposition, all contributed to Thatcher's second election victory in [1983](/wiki/1983_United_Kingdom_general_election "1983 United Kingdom general election").[256] Thatcher often referred after the war to the "Falklands spirit".[257][258]

#### Negotiating Hong Kong

In September 1982, she visited China to discuss with [Deng Xiaoping](/wiki/Deng_Xiaoping "Deng Xiaoping") the [sovereignty of Hong Kong](/wiki/Sovereignty_of_Hong_Kong "Sovereignty of Hong Kong") after 1997. China was the first communist state Thatcher had visited as prime minister, and she was the first British prime minister to visit China. Throughout their meeting, she sought the PRC's agreement to a continued British presence in the territory. Deng insisted that the PRC's sovereignty over Hong Kong was non-negotiable but stated his willingness to settle the sovereignty issue with the British government through formal negotiations. Both governments promised to maintain Hong Kong's stability and prosperity.[259] After the two-year negotiations, Thatcher conceded to the PRC government and signed the [Sino-British Joint Declaration](/wiki/Sino-British_Joint_Declaration "Sino-British Joint Declaration") in Beijing in 1984, agreeing to hand over Hong Kong's sovereignty in 1997.[260]

#### Apartheid in South Africa

Despite saying that she was in favour of "peaceful negotiations" to end [apartheid](/wiki/Apartheid "Apartheid"),[261][262] Thatcher opposed [sanctions imposed on South Africa](/wiki/Disinvestment_from_South_Africa "Disinvestment from South Africa") by the Commonwealth and the [European Economic Community](/wiki/European_Economic_Community "European Economic Community") (EEC).[263] She attempted to preserve trade with South Africa while persuading its government to abandon apartheid. This included "[c]asting herself as [President Botha](/wiki/President_Botha "President Botha")'s candid friend" and inviting him to visit the UK in 1984,[264] despite the "inevitable demonstrations" against his government.[265] Alan Merrydew of the Canadian broadcaster [BCTV News](/wiki/BCTV_News "BCTV News") asked Thatcher what her response was "to a reported ANC statement that they will target British firms in South Africa?" to which she later replied: "[...] when the ANC says that they will target British companies [...] This shows what a typical terrorist organisation it is. I fought terrorism all my life and if more people fought it, and we were all more successful, we should not have it and I hope that everyone in this hall will think it is right to go on fighting terrorism."[266] During his visit to Britain five months after his release from prison, [Nelson Mandela](/wiki/Nelson_Mandela "Nelson Mandela") praised Thatcher: "She is an enemy of apartheid [...] We have much to thank her for."[264]

#### Europe

See also: [Bruges speech](/wiki/Bruges_speech "Bruges speech")

External videos  
---  
1988 speech to the [College of Europe](/wiki/College_of_Europe "College of Europe")  
[](/wiki/File:Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg) [_Speech to the College of Europe_ ('The Bruges Speech')__](https://www.margaretthatcher.org/document/113688)(Speech)- via the Margaret Thatcher Foundation.[267]  
  
Thatcher and her party supported British membership of the EEC in the [1975 national referendum](/wiki/1975_United_Kingdom_European_Communities_membership_referendum "1975 United Kingdom European Communities membership referendum")[268] and the [Single European Act](/wiki/Single_European_Act "Single European Act") of 1986, and obtained the [UK rebate](/wiki/UK_rebate "UK rebate") on contributions,[269] but she believed that the role of the organisation should be limited to ensuring free trade and effective competition, and feared that the EEC approach was at odds with her views on smaller government and deregulation.[270] Believing that the single market would result in political integration,[269] Thatcher's opposition to further [European integration](/wiki/European_integration "European integration") became more pronounced during her premiership and particularly after her third government in 1987.[271] In her Bruges speech in 1988, Thatcher outlined her opposition to proposals from the EEC,[267] forerunner of the [European Union](/wiki/European_Union "European Union"), for a federal structure and increased centralisation of decision-making:

> We have not successfully rolled back the frontiers of the state in Britain, only to see them re-imposed at a European level, with a European super-state exercising a new dominance from Brussels.[270]

Sharing the concerns of French president [François Mitterrand](/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Mitterrand "François Mitterrand"),[272] Thatcher was initially opposed to [German reunification](/wiki/German_reunification "German reunification"),[nb 9] telling Gorbachev that it "would lead to a change to postwar borders, and we cannot allow that because such a development would undermine the stability of the whole international situation and could endanger our security". She expressed concern that a united Germany would align itself more closely with the Soviet Union and move away from NATO.[274]

In March 1990, Thatcher held a Chequers seminar on the subject of German reunification that was attended by members of her cabinet and historians such as [Norman Stone](/wiki/Norman_Stone "Norman Stone"), [George Urban](/wiki/George_Urban "George Urban"), [Timothy Garton Ash](/wiki/Timothy_Garton_Ash "Timothy Garton Ash") and [Gordon A. Craig](/wiki/Gordon_A._Craig "Gordon A. Craig"). During the seminar, Thatcher described "what Urban called 'saloon bar [clichés](/wiki/Clich%C3%A9 "Cliché")' about the German character, including '[angst](/wiki/Angst "Angst"), aggressiveness, [assertiveness](/wiki/Assertiveness "Assertiveness"), bullying, [egotism](/wiki/Egotism "Egotism"), [inferiority complex](/wiki/Inferiority_complex "Inferiority complex") [and] [sentimentality](/wiki/Sentimentality "Sentimentality")'". Those present were shocked to hear Thatcher's utterances and "appalled" at how she was "apparently unaware" about the post-war [German collective guilt](/wiki/German_collective_guilt "German collective guilt") and Germans' attempts to [work through their past](/wiki/Vergangenheitsbew%C3%A4ltigung "Vergangenheitsbewältigung").[275] The words of the meeting were leaked by her foreign-policy advisor [Charles Powell](/wiki/Charles_Powell,_Baron_Powell_of_Bayswater "Charles Powell, Baron Powell of Bayswater") and, subsequently, her comments met fierce backlash and controversy.[276]

During the same month, German chancellor [Helmut Kohl](/wiki/Helmut_Kohl "Helmut Kohl") reassured Thatcher that he would keep her "informed of all his intentions about unification",[277] and that he was prepared to disclose "matters which even his cabinet would not know".[277]

### Challenges to leadership and resignation

Main articles: [1989 Conservative Party leadership election](/wiki/1989_Conservative_Party_leadership_election "1989 Conservative Party leadership election") and [1990 Conservative Party leadership election](/wiki/1990_Conservative_Party_leadership_election "1990 Conservative Party leadership election")

[](/wiki/File:Thatcher_reviews_troops.jpg)Reviewing the [Royal Bermuda Regiment](/wiki/Royal_Bermuda_Regiment "Royal Bermuda Regiment") in 1990

During her premiership, Thatcher had the second-lowest average approval rating (40%) of any post-war prime minister. Since Nigel Lawson's resignation as chancellor in October 1989,[278] polls consistently showed that she was less popular than her party.[279] A self-described conviction politician, Thatcher always insisted that she did not care about her poll ratings and pointed instead to her unbeaten election record.[280]

In December 1989, Thatcher was challenged for the leadership of the Conservative Party by the little-known backbench MP [Sir Anthony Meyer](/wiki/Sir_Anthony_Meyer "Sir Anthony Meyer").[281] Of the 374 Conservative MPs eligible to vote, 314 voted for Thatcher and 33 for Meyer. Her supporters in the party viewed the result as a success and rejected suggestions that there was discontent within the party.[281]

Opinion polls in September 1990 reported that Labour had established a 14% lead over the Conservatives,[282] and by November, the Conservatives had been trailing Labour for 18 months.[279] These ratings, together with Thatcher's combative personality and tendency to override collegiate opinion, contributed to further discontent within her party.[283]

In July 1989, Thatcher removed Geoffrey Howe as [foreign secretary](/wiki/Foreign_secretary_\(United_Kingdom\) "Foreign secretary \(United Kingdom\)") after he and Lawson had forced her to agree to a plan for Britain to join the [European Exchange Rate Mechanism](/wiki/European_Exchange_Rate_Mechanism "European Exchange Rate Mechanism") (ERM). Britain joined the ERM in October 1990. 

On 1 November 1990, Howe, by then the last remaining member of Thatcher's original 1979 cabinet, resigned as [deputy prime minister](/wiki/Deputy_Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom "Deputy Prime Minister of the United Kingdom"), ostensibly over her open hostility to moves towards [European monetary union](/wiki/European_monetary_union "European monetary union").[282][284] In his resignation speech on 13 November, which was instrumental in Thatcher's downfall,[285] Howe attacked Thatcher's openly dismissive attitude to the government's proposal for a new European currency competing against existing currencies (a "[hard ECU](/wiki/Hard_ECU "Hard ECU")"): 

> How on earth are the Chancellor and the Governor of the Bank of England, commending the hard ECU as they strive to, to be taken as serious participants in the debate against that kind of background noise? I believe that both the Chancellor and the Governor are cricketing enthusiasts, so I hope that there is no monopoly of cricketing metaphors. It is rather like sending your opening batsmen to the crease only for them to find, the moment the first balls are bowled, that their bats have been broken before the game by the team captain.[286][287]

On 14 November, Michael Heseltine mounted a challenge for the leadership of the Conservative Party.[288][289] Opinion polls had indicated that he would give the Conservatives a national lead over Labour.[290] Although Thatcher led on the first ballot with the votes of 204 Conservative MPs (54.8%) to 152 votes (40.9%) for Heseltine, with 16 abstentions, she was four votes short of the required 15% majority. A second ballot was therefore necessary.[291] Thatcher initially declared her intention to "fight on and fight to win" the second ballot, but consultation with her cabinet persuaded her to withdraw.[283][292] After holding an audience with the Queen, calling other world leaders, and making one final Commons speech,[293] on 28 November she left Downing Street in tears. She reportedly regarded her ousting as a betrayal.[294] Her resignation was a shock to many outside Britain, with such foreign observers as [Henry Kissinger](/wiki/Henry_Kissinger "Henry Kissinger") and Gorbachev expressing private consternation.[295]

Chancellor John Major replaced Thatcher as head of government and party leader, whose lead over Heseltine in the second ballot was sufficient for Heseltine to drop out. Major oversaw an upturn in Conservative support in the 17 months leading to the [1992 general election](/wiki/1992_United_Kingdom_general_election "1992 United Kingdom general election") and led the party to a fourth successive victory on 9 April 1992.[296] Thatcher had lobbied for Major in the leadership contest against Heseltine, but her support for him waned in later years.[297]

## Later life

### Return to backbenches (1990–1992)

After leaving the premiership, Thatcher returned to the backbenches as a constituency parliamentarian.[298] Her domestic approval rating recovered after her resignation, though public opinion remained divided on whether her government had been good for the country.[278][299] Aged 66, she retired from the House of Commons at the 1992 general election, saying that leaving the Commons would allow her more freedom to speak her mind.[300]

### Post-Commons (1992–2003)

On leaving the Commons, Thatcher became the first former British prime minister to set up a foundation;[301] the British wing of the Margaret Thatcher Foundation was dissolved in 2005 due to financial difficulties.[302] She wrote two volumes of memoirs, _[The Downing Street Years](/wiki/The_Downing_Street_Years "The Downing Street Years")_ (1993) and _[The Path to Power](/wiki/The_Path_to_Power_\(Thatcher_book\) "The Path to Power \(Thatcher book\)")_ (1995). In 1991, she and her husband Denis moved to a house in [Chester Square](/wiki/Chester_Square "Chester Square"), a residential garden square in central London's [Belgravia](/wiki/Belgravia "Belgravia") district.[303]

Thatcher was hired by the tobacco company [Philip Morris](/wiki/Altria "Altria") as a "geopolitical consultant" in July 1992 for $250,000 per year and an annual contribution of $250,000 to her foundation.[304] Thatcher earned $50,000 for each speech she delivered.[305]

Thatcher became an advocate of [Croatian](/wiki/Independence_of_Croatia "Independence of Croatia") and [Slovenian](/wiki/Independence_of_Slovenia "Independence of Slovenia") independence.[306] Commenting on the [Yugoslav Wars](/wiki/Yugoslav_Wars "Yugoslav Wars"), in a 1991 interview for [Croatian Radiotelevision](/wiki/Croatian_Radiotelevision "Croatian Radiotelevision"), she was critical of Western governments for not recognising the breakaway republics of Croatia and Slovenia as independent and for not supplying them with arms after the Serbian-led [Yugoslav Army](/wiki/Armed_Forces_of_Serbia_and_Montenegro "Armed Forces of Serbia and Montenegro") attacked.[307] In August 1992, she called for NATO to stop the Serbian assault on [Goražde](/wiki/Gora%C5%BEde "Goražde") and [Sarajevo](/wiki/Sarajevo "Sarajevo") to end [ethnic cleansing](/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing "Ethnic cleansing") during the [Bosnian War](/wiki/Bosnian_War "Bosnian War"), comparing the [situation in Bosnia–Herzegovina](/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing_in_the_Bosnian_War "Ethnic cleansing in the Bosnian War") to "the [barbarities of Hitler's and Stalin's](/wiki/Comparison_of_Nazism_and_Stalinism "Comparison of Nazism and Stalinism")".[308]

She made a series of speeches in the Lords criticising the [Maastricht Treaty](/wiki/Maastricht_Treaty "Maastricht Treaty"),[300] describing it as "a treaty too far" and stated: "I could never have signed this treaty."[309] She cited [A. V. Dicey](/wiki/A._V._Dicey "A. V. Dicey") when arguing that, as all three main parties were in favour of the treaty, the people should have their say in a referendum.[310]

Thatcher served as honorary [chancellor of the College of William & Mary](/wiki/Chancellor_of_the_College_of_William_%26_Mary "Chancellor of the College of William & Mary") in Virginia from 1993 to 2000,[311] while also serving as chancellor of the private [University of Buckingham](/wiki/University_of_Buckingham "University of Buckingham") from 1992 to 1998,[312][313] a university she had formally opened in 1976 as the former education secretary.[313]

After [Tony Blair](/wiki/Tony_Blair "Tony Blair")'s [election as Labour Party leader](/wiki/1994_Labour_Party_leadership_election "1994 Labour Party leadership election") in 1994, Thatcher praised Blair as "probably the most formidable [Labour leader](/wiki/Leader_of_the_Labour_Party_\(UK\) "Leader of the Labour Party \(UK\)") since [Hugh Gaitskell](/wiki/Hugh_Gaitskell "Hugh Gaitskell")", adding: "I see a lot of socialism behind their front bench, but not in Mr Blair. I think he genuinely has moved."[314] Blair responded in kind: "She was a thoroughly determined person, and that is an admirable quality."[315]

In 1998, Thatcher called for the release of former Chilean dictator [Augusto Pinochet](/wiki/Augusto_Pinochet "Augusto Pinochet") when [Spain had him arrested](/wiki/Indictment_and_arrest_of_Augusto_Pinochet "Indictment and arrest of Augusto Pinochet") and sought to try him for human rights violations. She cited the help he gave Britain during the Falklands War.[316] In 1999, she visited him while he was under house arrest near London.[317] Pinochet was released in March 2000 on medical grounds by Home Secretary [Jack Straw](/wiki/Jack_Straw "Jack Straw").[318]

[](/wiki/File:Margaret_Thatcher.jpg)Touring the [Kennedy Space Center](/wiki/Kennedy_Space_Center "Kennedy Space Center") in 2001

At the [2001 general election](/wiki/2001_United_Kingdom_general_election "2001 United Kingdom general election"), Thatcher supported the Conservative campaign, as she had done in 1992 and 1997, and in the [Conservative leadership election](/wiki/2001_Conservative_Party_leadership_election "2001 Conservative Party leadership election") following its defeat, she endorsed [Iain Duncan Smith](/wiki/Iain_Duncan_Smith "Iain Duncan Smith") over [Kenneth Clarke](/wiki/Kenneth_Clarke "Kenneth Clarke").[319] In 2002 she encouraged [George W. Bush](/wiki/George_W._Bush "George W. Bush") to aggressively tackle the "unfinished business" of Iraq under Saddam Hussein,[320] and praised Blair for his "strong, bold leadership" in standing with Bush in the [Iraq War](/wiki/Iraq_War "Iraq War").[321]

Thatcher broached the same subject in her _[Statecraft: Strategies for a Changing World](/wiki/Statecraft:_Strategies_for_a_Changing_World "Statecraft: Strategies for a Changing World")_ , which was published in April 2002 and dedicated to Ronald Reagan, writing that there would be no peace in the Middle East until [Saddam](/wiki/Saddam "Saddam") was toppled. Her book also said that Israel must trade [land for peace](/wiki/Land_for_peace "Land for peace") and that the European Union (EU) was a "fundamentally unreformable", "classic utopian project, a monument to the vanity of intellectuals, a programme whose inevitable destiny is failure".[322] She argued that Britain should renegotiate its terms of membership or else [leave the EU](/wiki/Brexit "Brexit") and join the [North American Free Trade Area](/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Area "North American Free Trade Area").[323]

Following several small strokes, her doctors advised her not to engage in further public speaking.[324] In March 2002 she announced that, on doctors' advice, she would cancel all planned speaking engagements and accept no more.[325]

Extract from _[The Downing Street Years](/wiki/The_Downing_Street_Years "The Downing Street Years")_

> "Being Prime Minister is a lonely job. In a sense, it ought to be: you cannot lead from the crowd. But with Denis there I was never alone. What a man. What a husband. What a friend."

— Thatcher (1993, p. 23)

On 26 June 2003, Thatcher's husband, Sir Denis, died aged 88;[326] his body was cremated on 3 July at [Mortlake Crematorium](/wiki/Mortlake_Crematorium "Mortlake Crematorium") in London.[327]

### Final years (2003–2013)

[](/wiki/File:Margaret_Thatcher_DF-SD-06-15534.jpg)Arriving for the funeral of President Reagan in 2004

On 11 June 2004, Thatcher (against doctors' orders) attended the [state funeral service for Ronald Reagan](/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Ronald_Reagan "Death and state funeral of Ronald Reagan").[328] She delivered her eulogy via videotape; in view of her health, the message had been pre-recorded several months earlier.[329][330] Thatcher flew to California with the Reagan entourage, and attended the memorial service and interment ceremony for the president at the [Ronald Reagan Presidential Library](/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_Presidential_Library "Ronald Reagan Presidential Library").[331]

In 2005, Thatcher criticised how Blair had decided to [invade Iraq](/wiki/2003_invasion_of_Iraq "2003 invasion of Iraq") two years previously. Although she still supported the intervention to topple Saddam Hussein, she said that (as a scientist) she would always look for "facts, evidence and proof" before committing the armed forces.[246] She celebrated her 80th birthday on 13 October at the [Mandarin Oriental Hotel](/wiki/Mandarin_Oriental_Hyde_Park,_London "Mandarin Oriental Hyde Park, London") in [Hyde Park, London](/wiki/Hyde_Park,_London "Hyde Park, London"); guests included the Queen, the [Duke of Edinburgh](/wiki/Prince_Philip,_Duke_of_Edinburgh "Prince Philip, Duke of Edinburgh"), [Princess Alexandra](/wiki/Princess_Alexandra,_The_Honourable_Lady_Ogilvy "Princess Alexandra, The Honourable Lady Ogilvy") and Tony Blair.[332] [Geoffrey Howe, Baron Howe of Aberavon](/wiki/Geoffrey_Howe,_Baron_Howe_of_Aberavon "Geoffrey Howe, Baron Howe of Aberavon"), was also in attendance and said of his former leader: "Her real triumph was to have transformed not just one party but two, so that when Labour did eventually return, the great bulk of Thatcherism was accepted as irreversible."[333]

In the US, 2006

[](/wiki/File:Thatcher_2006_September_11_event.jpg)

Thatcher (_left_) at a Washington memorial service on the fifth anniversary of the 9/11 attacks

[](/wiki/File:Margaret_Thatcher_060912-F-0193C-006.jpg)

With [Donald Rumsfeld](/wiki/Donald_Rumsfeld "Donald Rumsfeld") and [General Pace](/wiki/General_Pace "General Pace") at [the Pentagon](/wiki/The_Pentagon "The Pentagon")

In 2006, Thatcher attended the [official Washington memorial service](/wiki/Patriot_Day "Patriot Day") to commemorate the fifth anniversary of the [9/11 attacks](/wiki/9/11_attacks "9/11 attacks") on the US. She was a guest of Vice President [Dick Cheney](/wiki/Dick_Cheney "Dick Cheney") and met Secretary of State [Condoleezza Rice](/wiki/Condoleezza_Rice "Condoleezza Rice") during her visit.[334] In February 2007 Thatcher became the first living British prime minister to be honoured with [a statue](/wiki/Statue_of_Margaret_Thatcher_\(Palace_of_Westminster\) "Statue of Margaret Thatcher \(Palace of Westminster\)") in the [Houses of Parliament](/wiki/Houses_of_Parliament "Houses of Parliament"). The bronze statue stood opposite [that of her political hero, Winston Churchill](/wiki/Statue_of_Winston_Churchill,_Palace_of_Westminster "Statue of Winston Churchill, Palace of Westminster"),[335] and was unveiled on 21 February 2007 with Thatcher in attendance; she remarked in the [Members' Lobby](/wiki/Members%27_Lobby "Members' Lobby") of the Commons: "I might have preferred iron – but bronze will do [...] It won't rust."[335]

Thatcher was a public supporter of the [Prague Declaration on European Conscience and Communism](/wiki/Prague_Declaration_on_European_Conscience_and_Communism "Prague Declaration on European Conscience and Communism") and the resulting Prague Process and sent a public letter of support to its preceding conference.[336]

After collapsing at a [House of Lords](/wiki/House_of_Lords "House of Lords") dinner, Thatcher, suffering [low blood pressure](/wiki/Low_blood_pressure "Low blood pressure"),[337] was admitted to [St Thomas' Hospital](/wiki/St_Thomas%27_Hospital "St Thomas' Hospital") in central London on 7 March 2008 for tests. In 2009 she was hospitalised again when she fell and broke her arm.[338] Thatcher returned to 10 Downing Street in late November 2009 for the unveiling of [an official portrait](/wiki/Portrait_of_Margaret_Thatcher "Portrait of Margaret Thatcher") by artist [Richard Stone](/wiki/Richard_Stone_\(painter\) "Richard Stone \(painter\)"),[339] an unusual honour for a living former prime minister. Stone was previously commissioned to paint portraits of the Queen and [the Queen Mother](/wiki/The_Queen_Mother "The Queen Mother").[339]

On 4 July 2011, Thatcher was to attend a ceremony for the unveiling of a 10 ft (3.0 m) statue of Ronald Reagan outside the [US embassy in London](/wiki/US_embassy_in_London "US embassy in London"), but was unable to attend due to her frail health.[340] She last attended a sitting of the House of Lords on 19 July 2010,[341] and on 30 July 2011 it was announced that her office in the Lords had been closed.[1] Earlier that month, Thatcher was named the most competent prime minister of the past 30 years in an [Ipsos MORI](/wiki/Ipsos_MORI "Ipsos MORI") poll.[342]

Thatcher's daughter Carol [first revealed](https://en.wikinews.org/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher_suffering_from_dementia,_says_daughter "n:Margaret Thatcher suffering from dementia, says daughter") that her mother had [dementia](/wiki/Dementia "Dementia") in 2005,[343] saying "Mum doesn't read much any more because of her memory loss". In her 2008 memoir, Carol wrote that her mother "could hardly remember the beginning of a sentence by the time she got to the end".[343] She later recounted how she was first struck by her mother's dementia when, in conversation, Thatcher confused the Falklands and Yugoslav conflicts; she recalled the pain of needing to tell her mother repeatedly that her husband Denis was dead.[344]

### Death and funeral (2013)

Main article: [Death and funeral of Margaret Thatcher](/wiki/Death_and_funeral_of_Margaret_Thatcher "Death and funeral of Margaret Thatcher")

[](/wiki/File:Margaret_Thatcher_coffin_at_St._Pauls_X8A2604.jpg)

Thatcher's coffin being carried up the steps of [St Paul's Cathedral](/wiki/St_Paul%27s_Cathedral "St Paul's Cathedral")

[](/wiki/File:MTgrave1.jpeg)

Plaques on the graves of Margaret and Denis Thatcher at the [Royal Hospital Chelsea](/wiki/Royal_Hospital_Chelsea "Royal Hospital Chelsea")

Thatcher died on 8 April 2013, aged 87, after suffering a stroke. She had been staying in a suite at [the Ritz Hotel](/wiki/The_Ritz_Hotel,_London "The Ritz Hotel, London") in London since December 2012, having experienced difficulty with the stairs at her Chester Square home in Belgravia.[345] Her death certificate recorded the primary causes of death as a "cerebrovascular accident" and "repeated [transient ischaemic attack](/wiki/Transient_ischaemic_attack "Transient ischaemic attack")";[346] secondary causes were listed as "[carcinoma](/wiki/Carcinoma "Carcinoma") of the bladder" and dementia.[346]

[Reactions across the UK](https://en.wikiquote.org/wiki/Death_and_funeral_of_Margaret_Thatcher "wikiquote:Death and funeral of Margaret Thatcher") were sharply divided, ranging from tributes describing her as Britain's greatest peacetime prime minister to public celebrations of her death and expressions of intense personal hostility.[347]

Arrangements for Thatcher's funeral had been settled with her in advance.[348] She received a [ceremonial funeral](/wiki/Ceremonial_funeral "Ceremonial funeral") with full military honours, including a church service at [St Paul's Cathedral](/wiki/St_Paul%27s_Cathedral "St Paul's Cathedral") on 17 April.[349][350] Queen Elizabeth II and the Duke of Edinburgh attended,[351] the second and final time during her reign that she attended the funeral of one of [her former prime ministers](/wiki/List_of_prime_ministers_of_Elizabeth_II "List of prime ministers of Elizabeth II"), the first being [Churchill's state funeral](/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Winston_Churchill "Death and state funeral of Winston Churchill") in 1965.[352]

Following the service at St Paul's, Thatcher's body was cremated at Mortlake, where her husband had also been cremated. On 28 September, a service was held in the All Saints Chapel of the [Royal Hospital Chelsea](/wiki/Royal_Hospital_Chelsea "Royal Hospital Chelsea")'s Margaret Thatcher Infirmary, after which her ashes were interred privately in the hospital grounds beside her husband's.[353][354]

Thatcherism represented a systematic and decisive overhaul of the [post-war consensus](/wiki/Post-war_consensus "Post-war consensus"), whereby the major political parties largely agreed on the central themes of [Keynesianism](/wiki/Keynesianism "Keynesianism"), the [welfare state](/wiki/Welfare_state_in_the_United_Kingdom "Welfare state in the United Kingdom"), nationalised industry, and close regulation of the economy, and high taxes. Thatcher generally supported the welfare state while proposing to rid it of abuses.[nb 10]

She promised in 1982 that the highly popular [National Health Service](/wiki/National_Health_Service "National Health Service") was "safe in our hands".[355] At first, she ignored the question of privatising nationalised industries; heavily influenced by right-wing think tanks, and especially by [Sir Keith Joseph](/wiki/Sir_Keith_Joseph "Sir Keith Joseph"),[356] Thatcher broadened her attack. Thatcherism came to refer to her policies as well as aspects of her ethical outlook and personal style, including [moral absolutism](/wiki/Moral_absolutism "Moral absolutism"), [nationalism](/wiki/Nationalism "Nationalism"), [liberal individualism](/wiki/Liberal_individualism "Liberal individualism"), and an uncompromising approach to achieving political goals.[357][358][nb 11]

Thatcher defined her political philosophy, in a major and controversial break with the [one-nation conservatism](/wiki/One-nation_conservatism "One-nation conservatism")[359] of her predecessor Edward Heath, in a 1987 interview published in _[Woman's Own](/wiki/Woman%27s_Own "Woman's Own")_ magazine: 

> I think we have gone through a period when too many children and people have been given to understand "I have a problem, it is the Government's job to cope with it!" or "I have a problem, I will go and get a grant to cope with it!" "I am homeless, the Government must house me!" and so they are casting their problems on society and who is society? There is no such thing! There are individual men and women and there are families and no government can do anything except through people and people look to themselves first. It is our duty to look after ourselves and then also to help look after our neighbour and life is a reciprocal business and people have got the entitlements too much in mind without the obligations.[360]

#### Overview

The number of adults owning shares rose from 7 per cent to 25 per cent during her tenure, and more than a million families bought their council houses, increasing from 55 per cent to 67 per cent in [owner-occupiers](/wiki/Owner-occupier "Owner-occupier") from 1979 to 1990. The houses were sold at a discount of 33–55 per cent, leading to large profits for some new owners. Personal wealth rose by 80 per cent in real terms during the 1980s, mainly due to rising house prices and increased earnings. Shares in the privatised utilities were sold below their market value to ensure quick and wide sales rather than maximise national income.[361][362]

The "Thatcher years" were also marked by periods of high unemployment and social unrest,[363][364] and many critics on the left of the political spectrum fault her economic policies for the unemployment level; many of the areas affected by mass unemployment as well as her monetarist economic policies remained blighted for decades, by such social problems as [drug abuse](/wiki/Drug_abuse "Drug abuse") and family breakdown.[365] Unemployment did not fall below its May 1979 level during her tenure,[366] only falling below its April 1979 level in 1990.[367] The long-term effects of her policies on manufacturing remain contentious.[368][369]

Speaking in Scotland in 2009, Thatcher insisted she had no regrets and was right to introduce the poll tax and withdraw subsidies from "outdated industries, whose markets were in terminal decline", subsidies that created "the culture of dependency, which had done such damage to Britain".[370] Political economist [Susan Strange](/wiki/Susan_Strange "Susan Strange") termed the neoliberal financial growth model "casino capitalism", reflecting her view that speculation and financial trading were becoming more important to the economy than industry.[371]

Critics on the left describe her as divisive[372] and say she condoned greed and selfishness.[363] Leading Welsh politician [Rhodri Morgan](/wiki/Rhodri_Morgan "Rhodri Morgan"),[373] among others,[374] characterised Thatcher as a "[Marmite](/wiki/Marmite "Marmite")" figure. Journalist [Michael White](/wiki/Michael_White_\(journalist\) "Michael White \(journalist\)"), writing in the aftermath of the [2008 financial crisis](/wiki/2008_financial_crisis "2008 financial crisis"), challenged the view that her reforms were still a net benefit.[375] Others consider her approach to have been "a mixed bag"[376][377] and "[a] [Curate's egg](/wiki/Curate%27s_egg "Curate's egg")".[378]

Thatcher did "little to advance the political cause of women" within her party or the government.[379] Some [British feminists](/wiki/Feminism_in_the_United_Kingdom "Feminism in the United Kingdom") regarded her as "an enemy".[380] [June Purvis](/wiki/June_Purvis "June Purvis") in _[Women's History Review](/wiki/Women%27s_History_Review "Women's History Review")_ says that, although Thatcher had struggled laboriously against the sexist prejudices of her day to rise to the top, she made no effort to ease the path for other women.[381] Thatcher did not regard [women's rights](/wiki/Women%27s_rights "Women's rights") as requiring particular attention as she did not, especially during her premiership, consider that women were being deprived of their rights. She had once suggested the shortlisting of women by default for all public appointments and proposed that those with young children should leave the workforce.[382]

Thatcher's stance on [immigration](/wiki/Modern_immigration_to_the_United_Kingdom "Modern immigration to the United Kingdom") in the late 1970s was perceived as part of a rising racist public discourse,[383] which [Martin Barker](/wiki/Martin_Barker "Martin Barker") terms "[new racism](/wiki/New_racism "New racism")".[384] In opposition, Thatcher believed that the National Front (NF) was winning over large numbers of Conservative voters with warnings against floods of immigrants. Her strategy was to undermine the NF narrative by acknowledging that many of [their voters](/wiki/Support_base_of_the_National_Front_\(UK\) "Support base of the National Front \(UK\)") had serious concerns in need of addressing. In 1978 she criticised Labour's immigration policy to attract voters away from the NF to the Conservatives.[385] Her rhetoric was followed by increased Conservative support at the expense of the NF. Critics on the left accused her of [pandering](/wiki/Pandering_\(politics\) "Pandering \(politics\)") to racism.[386][nb 12]

Many Thatcherite policies influenced the Labour Party,[390][391] which returned to power in 1997 under Tony Blair. Blair rebranded the party "[New Labour](/wiki/New_Labour "New Labour")" in 1994 with the aim of increasing its appeal beyond its traditional supporters,[392] and to attract those who had supported Thatcher, such as the "[Essex man](/wiki/Essex_man "Essex man")".[393] Thatcher is said to have regarded the "New Labour" rebranding as her greatest achievement.[394] In contrast to Blair, the Conservative Party under [William Hague](/wiki/William_Hague "William Hague") attempted to distance himself and the party from Thatcher's economic policies in an attempt to gain public approval.[395]

Shortly after Thatcher died in 2013, Scottish first minister [Alex Salmond](/wiki/Alex_Salmond "Alex Salmond") argued that her policies had the "unintended consequence" of encouraging Scottish devolution.[396] [Lord Foulkes of Cumnock](/wiki/Lord_Foulkes_of_Cumnock "Lord Foulkes of Cumnock") agreed on _[Scotland Tonight](/wiki/Scotland_Tonight "Scotland Tonight")_ that she had provided "the impetus" for devolution.[397] Writing for _[The Scotsman](/wiki/The_Scotsman "The Scotsman")_ in 1997, Thatcher argued against devolution on the basis that it would eventually lead to [Scottish independence](/wiki/Scottish_independence "Scottish independence").[398]

#### Reputation

> Margaret Thatcher was not merely the first woman and the longest-serving Prime Minister of modern times, but the most admired, most hated, most idolised and most vilified public figure of the second half of the twentieth century. To some she was the saviour of her country who [...] created a vigorous enterprise economy which twenty years later was still outperforming the more regulated economies of the Continent. To others, she was a narrow ideologue whose hard-faced policies legitimised greed, deliberately increased inequality [...] and destroyed the nation's sense of solidarity and civic pride. There is no reconciling these views: yet both are true.[nb 13]

— Biographer [John Campbell](/wiki/John_Campbell_\(biographer\) "John Campbell \(biographer\)") (2011b, p. 499)

Thatcher's [tenure of 11 years and 209 days](/wiki/List_of_prime_ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom_by_length_of_tenure "List of prime ministers of the United Kingdom by length of tenure") as British prime minister was the longest since [Lord Salisbury](/wiki/Lord_Salisbury "Lord Salisbury") in the late 19th century (13 years and 252 days, in three spells) and the longest continuous period in office since [Lord Liverpool](/wiki/Lord_Liverpool "Lord Liverpool") in the early 19th century (14 years and 305 days).[399][400]

Having led the Conservative Party to victory in three consecutive general elections, twice in a landslide, she ranks among the most popular party leaders in British history regarding votes cast for the winning party; over 40 million ballots were cast in total for the party under her leadership.[401][402][403] Her electoral successes were dubbed a "historic [hat trick](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/hat_trick "wikt:hat trick")" by the British press in 1987.[404]

Thatcher ranked highest among living persons in the 2002 BBC poll _[100 Greatest Britons](/wiki/100_Greatest_Britons "100 Greatest Britons")_.[405] In 1999, [_Time_](/wiki/Time_\(magazine\) "Time \(magazine\)") deemed Thatcher one of the [100 most important people of the 20th century](/wiki/Time_100:_The_Most_Important_People_of_the_Century "Time 100: The Most Important People of the Century").[406] In 2015 she topped a poll by [Scottish Widows](/wiki/Scottish_Widows "Scottish Widows"), a major financial services company, as the most influential woman of the past 200 years;[407] and in 2016 topped [BBC Radio 4](/wiki/BBC_Radio_4 "BBC Radio 4")'s _[Woman's Hour](/wiki/Woman%27s_Hour "Woman's Hour") Power List_ of women judged to have had the biggest impact on female lives over the past 70 years.[408][409] In 2020, _Time_ magazine included Thatcher's name on its list of 100 Women of the Year. She was chosen as the Woman of the Year in 1982 when the Falklands War began under her command, resulting in the British victory.[410]

In contrast to her relatively poor average approval rating as prime minister,[299] Thatcher has since [ranked highly in retrospective opinion polling](/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom "Historical rankings of prime ministers of the United Kingdom") and, according to [YouGov](/wiki/YouGov "YouGov"), is "see[n] in overall positive terms" by the British public.[411] Just after her death in 2013, according to a poll by _[The Guardian](/wiki/The_Guardian "The Guardian")_ , about half of the public viewed her positively while one third viewed her negatively.[412] In a 2019 opinion poll by YouGov, most Britons rated her as Britain's greatest post-war leader (with Churchill coming second).[413] According to the poll, more than four in ten Britons (44%) think that Thatcher was a "good" or "great" prime minister, compared to 29% who think she was a "poor" or "terrible" one.[413] She was voted the fourth-greatest British prime minister of the 20th century in a 2011 poll of 139 academics organised by [MORI](/wiki/MORI "MORI").[414] In a 2016 [University of Leeds](/wiki/University_of_Leeds "University of Leeds") survey of 82 academics specialising in post-1945 British history and politics, she was voted the second-greatest British prime minister after the Second World War.[415]

### Cultural depictions

Main article: [Cultural depictions of Margaret Thatcher](/wiki/Cultural_depictions_of_Margaret_Thatcher "Cultural depictions of Margaret Thatcher")

According to theatre critic [Michael Billington](/wiki/Michael_Billington_\(critic\) "Michael Billington \(critic\)"),[416] Thatcher left an "emphatic mark" on the arts while prime minister.[417] One of the earliest satires of Thatcher as prime minister involved satirist [John Wells](/wiki/John_Wells_\(satirist\) "John Wells \(satirist\)") (as writer and performer), actress [Janet Brown](/wiki/Janet_Brown "Janet Brown") (voicing Thatcher) and future _[Spitting Image](/wiki/Spitting_Image "Spitting Image")_ producer [John Lloyd](/wiki/John_Lloyd_\(producer\) "John Lloyd \(producer\)") (as co-producer), who in 1979 were teamed up by producer [Martin Lewis](/wiki/Martin_Lewis_\(humorist\) "Martin Lewis \(humorist\)") for the satirical audio album [_The Iron Lady_](/wiki/The_Iron_Lady_\(album\) "The Iron Lady \(album\)"), which consisted of skits and songs satirising Thatcher's rise to power. The album was released in September 1979.[418][419] Thatcher was heavily satirised on _Spitting Image_ , and _[The Independent](/wiki/The_Independent "The Independent")_ labelled her "every stand-up's dream".[420]

Thatcher was the subject or the inspiration for 1980s [protest songs](/wiki/Protest_song "Protest song"). Musicians [Billy Bragg](/wiki/Billy_Bragg "Billy Bragg") and [Paul Weller](/wiki/Paul_Weller "Paul Weller") helped to form the [Red Wedge](/wiki/Red_Wedge "Red Wedge") collective to support Labour in opposition to Thatcher.[421] Known as "Maggie" by supporters and opponents alike, the chant song "[Maggie Out](/wiki/Maggie_Out "Maggie Out")" became a signature rallying cry among the left during the latter half of her premiership.[422]

Wells parodied Thatcher in several media. He collaborated with [Richard Ingrams](/wiki/Richard_Ingrams "Richard Ingrams") on the spoof "[Dear Bill](/wiki/Dear_Bill "Dear Bill")" letters, which ran as a column in _[Private Eye](/wiki/Private_Eye "Private Eye")_ magazine; they were also published in book form and became a West End stage revue titled _Anyone for Denis?_ , with Wells in the role of Thatcher's husband. It was followed by [a 1982 TV special](/wiki/Anyone_for_Denis%3F_\(video\) "Anyone for Denis? \(video\)") directed by [Dick Clement](/wiki/Dick_Clement "Dick Clement"), in which Thatcher was played by [Angela Thorne](/wiki/Angela_Thorne "Angela Thorne").[423]

Since her premiership, Thatcher has been portrayed in a number of television programmes, documentaries, films and plays.[424] She was portrayed by [Patricia Hodge](/wiki/Patricia_Hodge "Patricia Hodge") in [Ian Curteis](/wiki/Ian_Curteis "Ian Curteis")'s long unproduced _[The Falklands Play](/wiki/The_Falklands_Play "The Falklands Play")_ (2002) and by [Andrea Riseborough](/wiki/Andrea_Riseborough "Andrea Riseborough") in the TV film _[The Long Walk to Finchley](/wiki/The_Long_Walk_to_Finchley "The Long Walk to Finchley")_ (2008). She is the protagonist in two films, played by [Lindsay Duncan](/wiki/Lindsay_Duncan "Lindsay Duncan") in [_Margaret_](/wiki/Margaret_\(2009_film\) "Margaret \(2009 film\)") (2009) and by [Meryl Streep](/wiki/Meryl_Streep "Meryl Streep") in [_The Iron Lady_](/wiki/The_Iron_Lady_\(film\) "The Iron Lady \(film\)") (2011),[425] in which she is depicted as suffering from dementia or [Alzheimer's disease](/wiki/Alzheimer%27s_disease "Alzheimer's disease").[426] She is a main character in the [fourth season](/wiki/The_Crown_\(season_4\) "The Crown \(season 4\)") of _[The Crown](/wiki/The_Crown_\(TV_series\) "The Crown \(TV series\)")_ , played by [Gillian Anderson](/wiki/Gillian_Anderson "Gillian Anderson").[427] Thatcher has a supporting role in the 2024 biographical film _[Reagan](/wiki/Reagan_\(2024_film\) "Reagan \(2024 film\)")_ , played by [Lesley-Anne Down](/wiki/Lesley-Anne_Down "Lesley-Anne Down").[428]

## Titles, awards and honours

Main article: [List of awards and honours received by Margaret Thatcher](/wiki/List_of_awards_and_honours_received_by_Margaret_Thatcher "List of awards and honours received by Margaret Thatcher")

[](/wiki/File:Margaret_Thatcher_awarded_Presidential_Medal_of_Freedom.jpg)Receiving the [Presidential Medal of Freedom](/wiki/Presidential_Medal_of_Freedom "Presidential Medal of Freedom") in 1991

Thatcher became a [privy counsellor](/wiki/Privy_Council_\(United_Kingdom\) "Privy Council \(United Kingdom\)") (PC) on becoming a secretary of state in 1970.[429] She was the first woman entitled to full membership rights as an honorary member of the [Carlton Club](/wiki/Carlton_Club "Carlton Club") on becoming Conservative Party leader in 1975.[430]

As prime minister, Thatcher received two honorary distinctions: 

  * 24 October 1979 (1979-10-24): Honorary Fellowship of the [Royal Institute of Chemistry](/wiki/Royal_Institute_of_Chemistry "Royal Institute of Chemistry") ([HonFRIC](/wiki/Honorary_Fellow_of_the_Royal_Institute_of_Chemistry "Honorary Fellow of the Royal Institute of Chemistry")),[431] which was merged into the [Royal Society of Chemistry](/wiki/Royal_Society_of_Chemistry "Royal Society of Chemistry") the following year;[432] and
  * 1 July 1983 (1983-07-01): Fellowship of the [Royal Society](/wiki/Royal_Society "Royal Society") ([FRS](/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society "Fellow of the Royal Society")), a point of controversy among some of the then Fellows.[433]



[Orders of chivalry](/wiki/Orders_of_chivalry "Orders of chivalry")

[](/wiki/File:Order_of_the_Garter_UK_ribbon.svg)

[Garter](/wiki/Order_of_the_Garter "Order of the Garter")   
UK, 1995   


[](/wiki/File:Ord.GoodHope-ribbon.gif)

[Good Hope](/wiki/Order_of_Good_Hope "Order of Good Hope")   
RSA, 1991   


[](/wiki/File:Gal%C3%B3_de_l%27Orde_del_M%C3%A8rit_\(UK\).svg)

[Merit](/wiki/Order_of_Merit "Order of Merit")   
UK, 1990   


[](/wiki/File:Order_of_St_John_\(UK\)_ribbon_-vector.svg)

[St John](/wiki/Order_of_Saint_John_\(chartered_1888\) "Order of Saint John \(chartered 1888\)")   
UK, 1991   


Shown are the ribbons for each order bestowed on Thatcher.

Two weeks after her resignation, Thatcher was appointed [Member of the Order of Merit](/wiki/Member_of_the_Order_of_Merit "Member of the Order of Merit") (OM) by Queen Elizabeth II. Thatcher's husband, Denis, was [made a hereditary baronet](/wiki/Thatcher_baronets "Thatcher baronets") at the same time;[434] as his wife, Thatcher was entitled to use the [honorific style](/wiki/Style_\(form_of_address\) "Style \(form of address\)") "Lady",[435] an automatically conferred title that she declined to use.[436][437][438] She would be made Lady Thatcher in her own right on her subsequent [ennoblement](/wiki/Ennoblement "Ennoblement").[439]

In the Falklands, [Margaret Thatcher Day](/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher_Day "Margaret Thatcher Day") has been marked each 10 January since 1992,[440] commemorating her first visit to the Islands in January 1983, six months after the [end of the Falklands War](/wiki/Aftermath_of_the_Falklands_War "Aftermath of the Falklands War") in June 1982.[441]

Thatcher became a member of the House of Lords in 1992 with a [life peerage](/wiki/Life_peer "Life peer") as Baroness Thatcher, of [Kesteven](/wiki/Kesteven "Kesteven") in the [County of Lincolnshire](/wiki/County_of_Lincolnshire "County of Lincolnshire").[300][442] Subsequently, the [College of Arms](/wiki/College_of_Arms "College of Arms") granted her use of a [personal coat of arms](/wiki/Armorial_of_prime_ministers_of_the_United_Kingdom "Armorial of prime ministers of the United Kingdom"); she was allowed to revise these arms, in 1995, on her appointment as [Lady Companion of the Order of the Garter](/wiki/Lady_Companion_of_the_Order_of_the_Garter "Lady Companion of the Order of the Garter") (LG), the UK's highest order of [chivalry](/wiki/Chivalry "Chivalry").[443]

Coats of arms of Baroness Thatcher  Pre–Garter appointment |  | Post–Garter appointment   
---|---|---  
[](/wiki/File:Coat_of_Arms_of_Margaret_Thatcher,_The_Baroness_Thatcher_\(1992%E2%80%931995\).svg "illustration") |  | [](/wiki/File:Coat_of_Arms_of_Margaret_Thatcher,_The_Baroness_Thatcher_\(1995%E2%80%932013\).svg "illustration") [](/wiki/File:Coat_of_Arms_of_Margaret_Thatcher,_The_Baroness_Thatcher_\(1995%E2%80%932013\)_\(Variant\).svg "illustration of variant")  
1992–1995 |  | [Lozenge](/wiki/Lozenge_\(heraldry\) "Lozenge \(heraldry\)"): 1995–2013  | [Escutcheon](/wiki/Escutcheon_\(heraldry\) "Escutcheon \(heraldry\)"): 1995–2013   
  
In the US, Thatcher received the [Ronald Reagan Freedom Award](/wiki/Ronald_Reagan_Freedom_Award "Ronald Reagan Freedom Award") from the [Reagan Presidential Foundation](/wiki/Reagan_Presidential_Foundation "Reagan Presidential Foundation") in 1998;[444] she was designated a patron of [the Heritage Foundation](/wiki/The_Heritage_Foundation "The Heritage Foundation") in 2006,[445][446] where she established the Margaret Thatcher Center for Freedom.[447]
